The next lemma gives the common tail-probability comparison betweenTmax∘T_{\max}^{\circ}and𝕋max∘\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}.

## Lemma 7.

SetΔQ:=maxa∈A⁡|Qaνa−1|.\Delta_{Q}:=\max_{a\in A}\left|\frac{Q_{a}}{\nu_{a}}-1\right|.

Ifℙ​(ΔQ>1/2)+ℙ​(C​γ​ΔQ​𝕋¯max∘>ηn)→0\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{Q}>1/2)+\mathbb{P}\!\left(C\gamma\Delta_{Q}\bar{\mathbb{T}}_{\max}^{\circ}>\eta_{n}\right)\to 0

for a universal constantC<∞C<\infty, andsupx∈ℝℙ​(|𝕋max∘−x|≤ηn)→0,\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}\!\left(|\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}-x|\leq\eta_{n}\right)\to 0,

thensupx∈ℝ|ℙ​(Tmax∘>x)−ℙ​(𝕋max∘>x)|=supx∈ℝ|ℙ​(Tmax∘≤x)−ℙ​(𝕋max∘≤x)|=o​(1).\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(T_{\max}^{\circ}>x)-\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}>x)\right|=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(T_{\max}^{\circ}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}\leq x)\right|=o(1).

## Proof.

On{ΔQ≤1/2}\{\Delta_{Q}\leq 1/2\},Qa,νa>0Q_{a},\nu_{a}>0for everya∈Aa\in A.
Lemma3, applied withsa=Sa∘s_{a}=S_{a}^{\circ},Va=QaV_{a}=Q_{a}, andV~a=νa\widetilde{V}_{a}=\nu_{a}, givesmaxa∈A⁡|Sa∘|I|1/2−γ​Qaγ−𝕋a∘|≤C​γ​ΔQ​𝕋¯max∘.\max_{a\in A}\left|\frac{S_{a}^{\circ}}{|I|^{1/2-\gamma}Q_{a}^{\gamma}}-\mathbb{T}_{a}^{\circ}\right|\leq C\gamma\Delta_{Q}\bar{\mathbb{T}}_{\max}^{\circ}.

Using|maxa⁡xa−maxa⁡ya|≤maxa⁡|xa−ya||\max_{a}x_{a}-\max_{a}y_{a}|\leq\max_{a}|x_{a}-y_{a}|gives|Tmax∘−𝕋max∘|≤C​γ​ΔQ​𝕋¯max∘.|T_{\max}^{\circ}-\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}|\leq C\gamma\Delta_{Q}\bar{\mathbb{T}}_{\max}^{\circ}.

Thusℙ​(|Tmax∘−𝕋max∘|>ηn)→0\mathbb{P}(|T_{\max}^{\circ}-\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}|>\eta_{n})\to 0under the
displayed assumptions. Proposition4, applied
withX=Tmax∘X=T_{\max}^{\circ}andY=𝕋max∘Y=\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}, proves the
distribution-function claim. The tail-probability claim is identical
because tail probabilities are complements of distribution functions.
∎

## B.2.3Approximating the bootstrap distribution by a fixed-denominator version

The proofs use the following auxiliary fixed-denominator bootstrap statisticsT0,a∗:=Sa∗|I|1/2−γ​Qaγ,T0,max∗:=maxa∈A⁡T0,a∗.T_{0,a}^{*}:=\frac{S_{a}^{*}}{|I|^{1/2-\gamma}Q_{a}^{\gamma}},\qquad T_{0,\max}^{*}:=\max_{a\in A}T_{0,a}^{*}.(B.16)

Conditionally on the data,(T0,a∗)a∈A(T_{0,a}^{*})_{a\in A}is Gaussian. The actual
bootstrap statistic implemented in (B.6) is not Gaussian,
because the same multipliers enter its numerator and denominator. But we
will show that the two versions have the same asymptotic bootstrap
distribution.

The following lemma is used in both regimes. It permits nonconstant means
through the indexrnr_{n}and therefore does not impose a bound on the level of
the mean.
It gives a uniform concentration bound for the random bootstrap denominator
and compares the random- and fixed-denominator bootstrap distribution
functions.

## Lemma 8.

## B.2.4Gaussian approximation of the fixed-denominator bootstrap distribution

Letωa:=(νa|I|)1/2−γ,𝕋a∘:=Sa∘|I|1/2−γ​νaγ=ωaSa∘νa.\omega_{a}:=\left(\frac{\nu_{a}}{|I|}\right)^{1/2-\gamma},\qquad\mathbb{T}_{a}^{\circ}:=\frac{S_{a}^{\circ}}{|I|^{1/2-\gamma}\nu_{a}^{\gamma}}=\omega_{a}\frac{S_{a}^{\circ}}{\sqrt{\nu_{a}}}.

Define𝕋∘:=(𝕋a∘)a∈A,𝕋max∘:=maxa∈A⁡𝕋a∘,𝕋¯max∘:=maxa∈A⁡|𝕋a∘|.\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{\circ}=(\mathbb{T}_{a}^{\circ})_{a\in A},\qquad\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}=\max_{a\in A}\mathbb{T}_{a}^{\circ},\qquad\bar{\mathbb{T}}_{\max}^{\circ}=\max_{a\in A}|\mathbb{T}_{a}^{\circ}|.(B.36)

LetΣ∘:=Cov​(𝕋∘),G∘∼N​(0,Σ∘).\Sigma_{\circ}:=\mathrm{Cov}(\mathbb{T}^{\circ}),\qquad G_{\circ}\sim N(0,\Sigma_{\circ}).

This definition includes all serial cross-covariances in Regime D. Under
Regime I independence it reduces to(Σ∘)a​b=∑t∈Ia∩Ib𝔼​[dt(ha)​dt(hb)]|Ia|1/2−γ​|Ib|1/2−γ​νaγ​νbγ.(\Sigma_{\circ})_{ab}=\frac{\sum_{t\in I_{a}\cap I_{b}}\mathbb{E}[d_{t}^{(h_{a})}d_{t}^{(h_{b})}]}{|I_{a}|^{1/2-\gamma}|I_{b}|^{1/2-\gamma}\nu_{a}^{\gamma}\nu_{b}^{\gamma}}.

WriteG∘,max:=maxa∈A⁡G∘,a,q∘,n,u:=inf{x∈ℝ:ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)≥u}.G_{\circ,\max}:=\max_{a\in A}G_{\circ,a},\qquad q_{\circ,n,u}:=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\geq u\}.

The following lemma shows that the fixed-denominator bootstrap approximation
follows from consistency of its conditional covariance matrix.
For matrices indexed byAA, write‖Σ‖max:=maxa,b∈A⁡|Σa​b|.\|\Sigma\|_{\max}:=\max_{a,b\in A}|\Sigma_{ab}|.

## Lemma 9.

Suppose that, for constants0<c<C<∞0<c<C<\infty,c≤mina∈A(Σ∘)a​a≤maxa∈A(Σ∘)a​a≤C,c\leq\min_{a\in A}(\Sigma_{\circ})_{aa}\leq\max_{a\in A}(\Sigma_{\circ})_{aa}\leq C,

and, with probability1−o​(1)1-o(1)over data,c≤mina∈A⁡Σ^a​a,maxa∈A⁡Σ^a​a≤C.c\leq\min_{a\in A}\widehat{\Sigma}_{aa},\qquad\max_{a\in A}\widehat{\Sigma}_{aa}\leq C.

If‖Σ^−Σ∘‖max=op​((log⁡p)−2),\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma_{\circ}\|_{\max}=o_{p}\bigl((\log p)^{-2}\bigr),(B.37)

thensupx∈ℝ|ℙ∗​(T0,max∗≤x)−ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)|=op​(1).\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}^{*}(T_{0,\max}^{*}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\right|=o_{p}(1).

## Proof.

LetΔΣ:=‖Σ^−Σ∘‖max.\Delta_{\Sigma}:=\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma_{\circ}\|_{\max}.

The covariance consistency assumption gives a deterministic sequenceεn↓0\varepsilon_{n}\downarrow 0such thatεn​(log⁡p)2→0,ℙ​(ΔΣ>εn)→0.\varepsilon_{n}(\log p)^{2}\to 0,\qquad\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{\Sigma}>\varepsilon_{n})\to 0.

On the event whereΔΣ≤εn\Delta_{\Sigma}\leq\varepsilon_{n}and the displayed
variance bounds forΣ^\widehat{\Sigma}hold, the conditional law of(T0,a∗)a∈A(T_{0,a}^{*})_{a\in A}is centered Gaussian with covarianceΣ^\widehat{\Sigma}, andG∘G_{\circ}is centered Gaussian with covarianceΣ∘\Sigma_{\circ}. Proposition25yieldssupx∈ℝ|ℙ∗​(T0,max∗≤x)−ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)|≤C​εn1/3​{1∨log⁡(p/εn)}2/3.\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}^{*}(T_{0,\max}^{*}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\right|\leq C\varepsilon_{n}^{1/3}\{1\vee\log(p/\varepsilon_{n})\}^{2/3}.

The right-hand side iso​(1)o(1)becauseεn​(log⁡p)2→0\varepsilon_{n}(\log p)^{2}\to 0andεn​{log⁡(1/εn)}2→0\varepsilon_{n}\{\log(1/\varepsilon_{n})\}^{2}\to 0. Since the event has
probability tending to one, the desired conclusion follows.

The next lemma transfers oracle Gaussian approximation and bootstrap
approximation into asymptotic size control.

## Lemma 10.

Fixα∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1). Under (B.5), suppose that the
conditions of Lemmas8and9hold. Suppose also thatsupx∈ℝ|ℙ​(Tmax∘≤x)−ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)|=o​(1).\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(T_{\max}^{\circ}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\right|=o(1).

Thenlim supn→∞ℙ​(Tmax>cn,1−α∗)≤α.\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max}>c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*}\bigr)\leq\alpha.

## Proof.

Lemmas8and9implyZn:=supx∈ℝ|ℙ∗​(Tmax∗≤x)−ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)|=op​(1).Z_{n}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}^{*}(T_{\max}^{*}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\right|=o_{p}(1).

Lemma5gives a deterministic sequenceρn↓0\rho_{n}\downarrow 0such thatℙ​(Zn>ρn)→0\mathbb{P}(Z_{n}>\rho_{n})\to 0. LetFn∗​(x):=ℙ∗​(Tmax∗≤x),En:={supx∈ℝ|Fn∗​(x)−ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)|≤ρn}.F_{n}^{*}(x):=\mathbb{P}^{*}\bigl(T_{\max}^{*}\leq x\bigr),\qquad E_{n}:=\left\{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\bigl|F_{n}^{*}(x)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\bigr|\leq\rho_{n}\right\}.

Thenℙ​(Enc)→0\mathbb{P}(E_{n}^{c})\to 0. OnEnE_{n},Fn∗​(cn,1−α∗)≥1−αF_{n}^{*}(c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*})\geq 1-\alpha, and henceℙ​(G∘,max≤cn,1−α∗)≥1−α−ρn.\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*})\geq 1-\alpha-\rho_{n}.

For all sufficiently largenn,ρn<1−α\rho_{n}<1-\alpha, socn,1−α∗≥q∘,n,1−α−ρnon​En.c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*}\geq q_{\circ,n,1-\alpha-\rho_{n}}\qquad\text{on }E_{n}.

Lemma6givesTmax≤Tmax∘T_{\max}\leq T_{\max}^{\circ}under (B.5). Thereforeℙ​(Tmax>cn,1−α∗)≤ℙ​(Tmax∘>q∘,n,1−α−ρn)+ℙ​(Enc).\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max}>c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*}\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max}^{\circ}>q_{\circ,n,1-\alpha-\rho_{n}}\bigr)+\mathbb{P}(E_{n}^{c}).

LetΔcdf,n:=supx∈ℝ|ℙ​(Tmax∘≤x)−ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)|.\Delta_{\mathrm{cdf},n}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(T_{\max}^{\circ}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\right|.

By assumption,Δcdf,n→0\Delta_{\mathrm{cdf},n}\to 0, soℙ​(Tmax∘>q∘,n,1−α−ρn)≤ℙ​(G∘,max>q∘,n,1−α−ρn)+Δcdf,n≤α+ρn+Δcdf,n.\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max}^{\circ}>q_{\circ,n,1-\alpha-\rho_{n}}\bigr)\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl(G_{\circ,\max}>q_{\circ,n,1-\alpha-\rho_{n}}\bigr)+\Delta_{\mathrm{cdf},n}\leq\alpha+\rho_{n}+\Delta_{\mathrm{cdf},n}.

Taking the limit superior proves the claim.
∎

## B.2.5Roadmap of the proofs

The final goal in each regime islim supn→∞ℙ​(Tmax>cn,1−α∗)≤α∀α∈(0,1).\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max}>c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*}\bigr)\leq\alpha\qquad\forall\alpha\in(0,1).

By Lemma10, it is enough to showsupx∈ℝ|ℙ​(Tmax∘≤x)−ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)|→0\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(T_{\max}^{\circ}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\right|\to 0

and to verify the conditions of Lemmas8and9. The display is reduced by
Lemma7to provingsupx∈ℝ|ℙ​(𝕋max∘≤x)−ℙ​(G∘,max≤x)|→0.\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{T}_{\max}^{\circ}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ,\max}\leq x)\right|\to 0.(B.38)

The bootstrap part is reduced by Lemmas8and9to proving the covariance consistency
condition‖Σ^−Σ∘‖max=op​((log⁡p)−2).\|\widehat{\Sigma}-\Sigma_{\circ}\|_{\max}=o_{p}\bigl((\log p)^{-2}\bigr).(B.39)

All remaining work in Regimes I and D is therefore to verify the centered
Gaussian approximation (B.38), the covariance
consistency condition (B.39), and the
denominator and anti-concentration conditions required by Lemmas7,8, and9.

## B.3Regime I Validity

## B.3.1Additional assumptions for Regime I

We consider the following additional assumptions for Regime I.

## Assumption B.4.

The vectorsD1,…,DnD_{1},\dots,D_{n}are independent acrosstt.

## Assumption B.5.

The interval-wise mean is asymptotically flat:rn=o​((log⁡n)−2).r_{n}=o\bigl((\log n)^{-2}\bigr).

## Assumption B.6.

The minimal interval length grows at least polynomially innn:Lmin=ω​((log⁡n)7).L_{\min}=\omega\bigl((\log n)^{7}\bigr).

## Remark 1.

AssumptionB.5constrains theshapeof the mean on each scanned interval, not its level. Ifμt(h)≡−ch\mu_{t}^{(h)}\equiv-c_{h}is constant intt, thenrn=0r_{n}=0for everynnregardless of the magnitude ofchc_{h}.

## B.3.2Main theorem

## Theorem 11.

Fix the exponentγ∈[0,1/2]\gamma\in[0,1/2]. Assume AssumptionsB.1,B.2,B.3, andB.4–B.6.
Letcn,1−α∗c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*}be the conditional(1−α)(1-\alpha)-quantile
in (B.6). Then the Gaussian multiplier
bootstrap withΘn=In\Theta_{n}=I_{n}is asymptotically conservative: under
(B.5),lim supn→∞ℙ​(Tmax>cn,1−α∗)≤α∀α∈(0,1).\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max}>c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*}\bigr)\leq\alpha\qquad\forall\alpha\in(0,1).

Following the roadmap in SectionB.2.5, it remains to prove
(B.38) and
(B.39) and to verify the conditions in
Lemmas7–9.
In what follows, SectionB.3.3gives preliminary
results, SectionsB.3.4andB.3.5prove
(B.38) and
(B.39), respectively, and
SectionB.3.6completes the proof of Theorem11by verifying the conditions
in Lemmas7–9.
For SectionsB.3.4–B.3.6,
we assume AssumptionsB.1,B.2,B.3, andB.4–B.6all hold.

for some constantC<∞C<\infty. Sincep=|A|≤n2p=|A|\leq n^{2}, the choiceu=x+2​log⁡nu=x+2\log ngivesp​e−u≤e−xpe^{-u}\leq e^{-x}. Taking thisuuand applying a
union bound overa∈Aa\in Agives, for everyx≥1x\geq 1, with probability at
least1−2​e−x1-2e^{-x},maxa=(I,h)∈A⁡|∑t∈I(dt(h)​2−𝔼​[dt(h)​2])|νa≤C​maxa∈A⁡{x+2​log⁡n|Ia|+x+2​log⁡n|Ia|}.\max_{a=(I,h)\in A}\frac{\left|\sum_{t\in I}\bigl(d_{t}^{(h)2}-\mathbb{E}[d_{t}^{(h)2}]\bigr)\right|}{\nu_{a}}\leq C\max_{a\in A}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{x+2\log n}{|I_{a}|}}+\frac{x+2\log n}{|I_{a}|}\right\}.

## B.3.5Proof of the fixed-denominator bootstrap covariance

Fora=(I,h)a=(I,h)andb=(J,g)b=(J,g), setK:=I∩JK:=I\cap J.
Recall from (B.10) and (B.11) thatDt(h)−D¯a=dt,a+μt,aD_{t}^{(h)}-\bar{D}_{a}=d_{t,a}+\mu_{t,a}fort∈It\in I.
By direct calculation,Σ^a​b=Ω^a​b|Ia|1/2−γ​|Ib|1/2−γ​Qaγ​Qbγ.\widehat{\Sigma}_{ab}=\frac{\widehat{\Omega}_{ab}}{|I_{a}|^{1/2-\gamma}|I_{b}|^{1/2-\gamma}Q_{a}^{\gamma}Q_{b}^{\gamma}}.

HereΩ^a​b\displaystyle\widehat{\Omega}_{ab}:=∑t∈K(Dt(h)−D¯a)​(Dt(g)−D¯b).\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in K}\left(D_{t}^{(h)}-\bar{D}_{a}\right)\left(D_{t}^{(g)}-\bar{D}_{b}\right).

The target covariance entry is(Σ∘)a​b=Ωa​b|Ia|1/2−γ​|Ib|1/2−γ​νaγ​νbγ,Ωa​b:=∑t∈K𝔼​[dt(h)​dt(g)].(\Sigma_{\circ})_{ab}=\frac{\Omega_{ab}}{|I_{a}|^{1/2-\gamma}|I_{b}|^{1/2-\gamma}\nu_{a}^{\gamma}\nu_{b}^{\gamma}},\qquad\Omega_{ab}:=\sum_{t\in K}\mathbb{E}\bigl[d_{t}^{(h)}d_{t}^{(g)}\bigr].

HenceΣ^a​b−(Σ∘)a​b\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{ab}-(\Sigma_{\circ})_{ab}=Ω^a​b−Ωa​b|Ia|1/2−γ​|Ib|1/2−γ​Qaγ​Qbγ\displaystyle=\frac{\widehat{\Omega}_{ab}-\Omega_{ab}}{|I_{a}|^{1/2-\gamma}|I_{b}|^{1/2-\gamma}Q_{a}^{\gamma}Q_{b}^{\gamma}}(B.43)+(Σ∘)a​b​{(Qaνa)−γ​(Qbνb)−γ−1}.\displaystyle\quad+(\Sigma_{\circ})_{ab}\left\{\left(\frac{Q_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\right)^{-\gamma}\left(\frac{Q_{b}}{\nu_{b}}\right)^{-\gamma}-1\right\}.(B.44)

We bound the two terms uniformly.

Step 1: bounding (B.44).On{ΔQ≤1/2}\{\Delta_{Q}\leq 1/2\}, for everya,b∈Aa,b\in A,Qaνa,Qbνb∈[1/2,3/2],|(Qaνa)−γ​(Qbνb)−γ−1|≤C​(|Qaνa−1|+|Qbνb−1|)≤C​ΔQ.\frac{Q_{a}}{\nu_{a}},\frac{Q_{b}}{\nu_{b}}\in[1/2,3/2],\qquad\left|\left(\frac{Q_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\right)^{-\gamma}\left(\frac{Q_{b}}{\nu_{b}}\right)^{-\gamma}-1\right|\leq C\left(\left|\frac{Q_{a}}{\nu_{a}}-1\right|+\left|\frac{Q_{b}}{\nu_{b}}-1\right|\right)\leq C\Delta_{Q}.

Cauchy–Schwarz and AssumptionB.2give|Ωa​b|\displaystyle|\Omega_{ab}|≤(∑t∈K𝔼​[dt(h)​2])1/2​(∑t∈K𝔼​[dt(g)​2])1/2≤νa​νb,\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{t\in K}\mathbb{E}[d_{t}^{(h)2}]\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{t\in K}\mathbb{E}[d_{t}^{(g)2}]\right)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{\nu_{a}\nu_{b}},|(Σ∘)a​b|\displaystyle|(\Sigma_{\circ})_{ab}|≤(νa|I|)1/2−γ​(νb|J|)1/2−γ≤C.\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\nu_{a}}{|I|}\right)^{1/2-\gamma}\left(\frac{\nu_{b}}{|J|}\right)^{1/2-\gamma}\leq C.

Thereforemaxa,b⁡|(Σ∘)a​b​{(Qaνa)−γ​(Qbνb)−γ−1}|≤C​ΔQ=Op​(log⁡nLmin+rn),\max_{a,b}\left|(\Sigma_{\circ})_{ab}\left\{\left(\frac{Q_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\right)^{-\gamma}\left(\frac{Q_{b}}{\nu_{b}}\right)^{-\gamma}-1\right\}\right|\leq C\Delta_{Q}=O_{p}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}}+r_{n}\right),(B.45)

by Lemma13.

Step 2: bounding (B.43).ExpandΩ^a​b=∑t∈Kdt,a​dt,b+∑t∈Kdt,a​μt,b+∑t∈Kdt,b​μt,a+∑t∈Kμt,a​μt,b.\widehat{\Omega}_{ab}=\sum_{t\in K}d_{t,a}d_{t,b}+\sum_{t\in K}d_{t,a}\mu_{t,b}+\sum_{t\in K}d_{t,b}\mu_{t,a}+\sum_{t\in K}\mu_{t,a}\mu_{t,b}.(B.46)

We analyze the four pieces.

andmaxa,b⁡|S(K,h)∘|νa=Op​(log⁡n),maxa,b⁡|S(K,g)∘|νb=Op​(log⁡n),\max_{a,b}\frac{|S_{(K,h)}^{\circ}|}{\sqrt{\nu_{a}}}=O_{p}\bigl(\sqrt{\log n}\bigr),\quad\max_{a,b}\frac{|S_{(K,g)}^{\circ}|}{\sqrt{\nu_{b}}}=O_{p}\bigl(\sqrt{\log n}\bigr),

where the last line follows from Lemma12.

Proposition24therefore impliessupx∈ℝℙ​(|G∘,max−x|≤ηn)→0.\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}\bigl(|G_{\circ,\max}-x|\leq\eta_{n}\bigr)\to 0.(B.51)

Thus (B.38) and

We now complete the proof of Theorem11.

## B.4Regime D Validity

## B.4.1Additional assumptions for Regime D

We impose the following assumptions.

## Assumption B.7.

The centered vector sequencedt=(dt(1),…,dt(H))d_{t}=(d_{t}^{(1)},\ldots,d_{t}^{(H)}),t=1,…,nt=1,\ldots,n, is geometricallyα\alpha-mixing: there exist constantsK1>1K_{1}>1,K2>0K_{2}>0, andγmix>0\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}}>0such thatα​(ℓ):=sup1≤k≤n−ℓsupA∈σ​(d1,…,dk),B∈σ​(dk+ℓ,…,dn)|ℙ​(A∩B)−ℙ​(A)​ℙ​(B)|≤K1​e−K2​ℓγmix∀ℓ≥1.\alpha(\ell):=\sup_{1\leq k\leq n-\ell}\sup_{A\in\sigma(d_{1},\dots,d_{k}),\ B\in\sigma(d_{k+\ell},\dots,d_{n})}\bigl|\mathbb{P}(A\cap B)-\mathbb{P}(A)\mathbb{P}(B)\bigr|\leq K_{1}e^{-K_{2}\ell^{\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}}}}\qquad\forall\ell\geq 1.

## Assumption B.8.

The centered oracle coordinates are nondegenerate: there exist constants0<c−≤c+<∞0<c_{-}\leq c_{+}<\inftysuch thatc−≤Var​(Sa∘νa)≤c+∀a∈A.c_{-}\leq\mathrm{Var}\!\left(\frac{S_{a}^{\circ}}{\sqrt{\nu_{a}}}\right)\leq c_{+}\qquad\forall a\in A.

## Assumption B.9.

The kernel is symmetric, continuously differentiable, satisfiesK​(0)=1K(0)=1,supx∈ℝ|K′​(x)|<∞,\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|K^{\prime}(x)|<\infty,

and obeys|K​(x)|≤CK​(1+|x|)−ϑ∀x∈ℝ|K(x)|\leq C_{K}(1+|x|)^{-\vartheta}\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}

for some constantsCK<∞C_{K}<\inftyandϑ>1\vartheta>1.
The bandwidth satisfiesbn≍nρ,0<ρ<ϑ−13​ϑ−2.b_{n}\asymp n^{\rho},\quad 0<\rho<\frac{\vartheta-1}{3\vartheta-2}.

The matricesΘn​(t,u):=K​(t−ubn),1≤t,u≤n,\Theta_{n}(t,u):=K\!\left(\frac{t-u}{b_{n}}\right),\qquad 1\leq t,u\leq n,(B.52)

are positive semidefinite.

## B.4.2Main theorem

## Theorem 15.

Fix the exponentγ∈[0,1/2]\gamma\in[0,1/2]. Assume AssumptionsB.1,B.2,B.3,B.7–B.9,
andB.10–B.12.
Letcn,1−α∗c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*}be the conditional(1−α)(1-\alpha)-quantile
in (B.6). Then the Gaussian multiplier
bootstrap with covarianceΘn​(t,u)=K​((t−u)/bn)\Theta_{n}(t,u)=K((t-u)/b_{n})is asymptotically conservative: under
(B.5),lim supn→∞ℙ​(Tmax>cn,1−α∗)≤α∀α∈(0,1).\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\bigl(T_{\max}>c_{n,1-\alpha}^{*}\bigr)\leq\alpha\qquad\forall\alpha\in(0,1).

Following the roadmap in SectionB.2.5, we are left to prove
(B.38) and
(B.39) and verify the conditions in
Lemmas7–9.
In the following, SectionB.4.3provides
preliminary results, SectionB.4.4proves
(B.38),
SectionB.4.5proves
(B.39), and
SectionB.4.6completes the proof of
Theorem15by
verifying the conditions in Lemmas7–9.
For SectionsB.4.4–B.4.6,
we assume AssumptionsB.1,B.2,B.3,B.7–B.9,
andB.10–B.12all hold.

## B.4.3Preliminary bounds

## Lemma 16.

Under AssumptionB.9, there existsCΘ<∞C_{\Theta}<\inftysuch thatmax1≤t≤n​∑u=1n|Θn​(t,u)|≤CΘ​bn,max1≤t≤n​∑u=1nΘn​(t,u)2≤CΘ​bnfor all​n.\max_{1\leq t\leq n}\sum_{u=1}^{n}|\Theta_{n}(t,u)|\leq C_{\Theta}b_{n},\qquad\max_{1\leq t\leq n}\sum_{u=1}^{n}\Theta_{n}(t,u)^{2}\leq C_{\Theta}b_{n}\qquad\text{for all }n.

Consequently,|x⊤​Θn​y|≤CΘ​bn​‖x‖2​‖y‖2∀x,y∈ℝn.|x^{\top}\Theta_{n}y|\leq C_{\Theta}b_{n}\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert y\right\rVert_{2}\qquad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

## Proof.

Fixtt. Since|K​(x)|≤CK​(1+|x|)−ϑ|K(x)|\leq C_{K}(1+|x|)^{-\vartheta}withϑ>1\vartheta>1,∑u=1n|Θn​(t,u)|≤∑u=1nCK​(1+|t−u|bn)−ϑ≤CK​∑m∈ℤ(1+|m|bn)−ϑ.\sum_{u=1}^{n}|\Theta_{n}(t,u)|\leq\sum_{u=1}^{n}C_{K}\left(1+\frac{|t-u|}{b_{n}}\right)^{-\vartheta}\leq C_{K}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(1+\frac{|m|}{b_{n}}\right)^{-\vartheta}.

Split the last sum into|m|≤bn|m|\leq b_{n}and|m|>bn|m|>b_{n}.
The first part contributesO​(bn)O(b_{n}).
For the second part,∑|m|>bn(1+|m|bn)−ϑ≤C​bn​∑k≥1(1+k)−ϑ=O​(bn)\sum_{|m|>b_{n}}\left(1+\frac{|m|}{b_{n}}\right)^{-\vartheta}\leq Cb_{n}\sum_{k\geq 1}(1+k)^{-\vartheta}=O(b_{n})

becauseϑ>1\vartheta>1.
This proves the row-sum bound.
For the squared row-sum bound,∑u=1nΘn​(t,u)2≤C​∑m∈ℤ(1+|m|bn)−2​ϑ.\sum_{u=1}^{n}\Theta_{n}(t,u)^{2}\leq C\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(1+\frac{|m|}{b_{n}}\right)^{-2\vartheta}.

The terms with|m|≤bn|m|\leq b_{n}contributeO​(bn)O(b_{n}), while the terms with|m|>bn|m|>b_{n}are bounded byC​bn​∑k≥1(1+k)−2​ϑ=O​(bn)Cb_{n}\sum_{k\geq 1}(1+k)^{-2\vartheta}=O(b_{n})because2​ϑ>12\vartheta>1.
The quadratic-form bound follows from|x⊤​Θn​y|≤‖x‖2​‖Θn​y‖2≤‖x‖2​(maxt​∑u|Θn​(t,u)|)​‖y‖2.|x^{\top}\Theta_{n}y|\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{2}\,\left\lVert\Theta_{n}y\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{2}\,\left(\max_{t}\sum_{u}|\Theta_{n}(t,u)|\right)\left\lVert y\right\rVert_{2}.

∎

As in SectionB.3.4,‖Zt,a∘‖ψ2≤B0​(1∨σ−−1)​nLmin≲Bn∀t,a.\left\lVert Z_{t,a}^{\circ}\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq B_{0}(1\vee\sigma_{-}^{-1})\sqrt{\frac{n}{L_{\min}}}\lesssim B_{n}\qquad\forall t,a.

The sequence inherits the same geometric mixing rate. Furthermore,Var​(𝕋a∘)=(νa|I|)1−2​γ​Var​(Sa∘νa),\mathrm{Var}(\mathbb{T}_{a}^{\circ})=\left(\frac{\nu_{a}}{|I|}\right)^{1-2\gamma}\mathrm{Var}\!\left(\frac{S_{a}^{\circ}}{\sqrt{\nu_{a}}}\right),

so AssumptionsB.8andB.2bound every coordinate variance above and
away from zero.
Hence Proposition27applies under AssumptionB.10and givessupA∈ℛp|ℙ​(𝕋∘∈A)−ℙ​(G∘∈A)|→0.\sup_{A\in\mathcal{R}^{p}}\Bigl|\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{T}^{\circ}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(G_{\circ}\in A)\Bigr|\to 0.

Applying this withA=[−x,x]pA=[-x,x]^{p}yieldssupx∈ℝ|ℙ​(𝕋¯max∘≤x)−ℙ​(maxa∈A⁡|G∘,a|≤x)|→0.\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl|\mathbb{P}(\bar{\mathbb{T}}_{\max}^{\circ}\leq x)-\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{a\in A}|G_{\circ,a}|\leq x\right)\Bigr|\to 0.

Proposition33applied to(G∘,a)a∈A(G_{\circ,a})_{a\in A}givesmaxa∈A⁡|G∘,a|=Op​(log⁡n),\max_{a\in A}|G_{\circ,a}|=O_{p}\bigl(\sqrt{\log n}\bigr),

Under AssumptionsB.1,B.2, andB.7–B.12,ΔQ=Op​(log⁡nLmin+rn).\Delta_{Q}=O_{p}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}}+\sqrt{r_{n}}\right).

In particular,ΔQ=op​(1)\Delta_{Q}=o_{p}(1)andℙ​(ΔQ>1/2)→0\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{Q}>1/2)\to 0.

## Proof.

Lemma2expresses bothqa−νaq_{a}-\nu_{a}andQa−qaQ_{a}-q_{a}in terms ofqa∘q_{a}^{\circ},CaC_{a}, andνa,μ\nu_{a,\mu}. We control these three terms
uniformly.

Step 1: control ofqa∘νa\frac{q_{a}^{\circ}}{\nu_{a}}.By (B.13),qa∘=∑t∈I(dt(h)​2−𝔼​[dt(h)​2])−|I|​d¯a2.q_{a}^{\circ}=\sum_{t\in I}\Bigl(d_{t}^{(h)2}-\mathbb{E}[d_{t}^{(h)2}]\Bigr)-|I|\bar{d}_{a}^{2}.

By Lemma18, there exist constantsCA,cA>0C_{A},c_{A}>0such that, for every fixeda=(I,h)a=(I,h)and everyx≥1x\geq 1withx+2​log⁡n≤cA​|I|γmix/(2+γmix),x+2\log n\leq c_{A}|I|^{\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}}/(2+\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}})},

one hasℙ​(|∑t∈I(dt(h)​2−𝔼​[dt(h)​2])|>CA​|I|​(x+2​log⁡n))≤2​e−(x+2​log⁡n).\mathbb{P}\!\left(\left|\sum_{t\in I}\Bigl(d_{t}^{(h)2}-\mathbb{E}[d_{t}^{(h)2}]\Bigr)\right|>C_{A}\sqrt{|I|(x+2\log n)}\right)\leq 2e^{-(x+2\log n)}.

AssumptionB.12implieslog⁡n=o​(Lminγmix/(2+γmix)),\log n=o\bigl(L_{\min}^{\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}}/(2+\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}})}\bigr),

so the restriction is eventually satisfied uniformly over all intervals for
any constantxx.
A union bound over theppcoordinates, retaining the coordinate-specific
normalization, givesmaxa=(I,h)∈A⁡|∑t∈I(dt(h)​2−𝔼​[dt(h)​2])|νa=Op​(maxa∈A⁡log⁡n|Ia|)=Op​(log⁡nLmin).\max_{a=(I,h)\in A}\frac{\left|\sum_{t\in I}\bigl(d_{t}^{(h)2}-\mathbb{E}[d_{t}^{(h)2}]\bigr)\right|}{\nu_{a}}=O_{p}\!\left(\max_{a\in A}\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{|I_{a}|}}\right)=O_{p}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}}\right).

AssumptionB.2givesνa|I|≥σ−2,ωa=(νa|I|)1/2−γ≥(σ−2)1/2−γ,ωa−1≤C.\frac{\nu_{a}}{|I|}\geq\sigma_{-}^{2},\qquad\omega_{a}=\left(\frac{\nu_{a}}{|I|}\right)^{1/2-\gamma}\geq(\sigma_{-}^{2})^{1/2-\gamma},\qquad\omega_{a}^{-1}\leq C.

SinceSa∘/νa=ωa−1​𝕋a∘S_{a}^{\circ}/\sqrt{\nu_{a}}=\omega_{a}^{-1}\mathbb{T}_{a}^{\circ},
Lemma17givesmaxa∈A⁡(Sa∘)2|I|​νa≤C​(𝕋¯max∘)2Lmin=Op​(log⁡nLmin).\max_{a\in A}\frac{(S_{a}^{\circ})^{2}}{|I|\nu_{a}}\leq\frac{C(\bar{\mathbb{T}}_{\max}^{\circ})^{2}}{L_{\min}}=O_{p}\!\left(\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}\right).

Combining the last two displays with the decomposition ofqa∘q_{a}^{\circ}at
the start of this step givesmaxa∈A⁡|qa∘|νa=Op​(log⁡nLmin+log⁡nLmin).\max_{a\in A}\frac{|q_{a}^{\circ}|}{\nu_{a}}=O_{p}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}}+\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}\right).(B.56)

Step 2: control ofCaνa\frac{C_{a}}{\nu_{a}}.The equality∑t∈Iμt,a=0\sum_{t\in I}\mu_{t,a}=0givesCa=∑t∈Idt,a​μt,aC_{a}=\sum_{t\in I}d_{t,a}\mu_{t,a}. Hence Cauchy–Schwarz yields the
pathwise bound|Ca|νa≤(qaνa)1/2​(νa,μνa)1/2≤rn​(qaνa)1/2.\frac{|C_{a}|}{\nu_{a}}\leq\left(\frac{q_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{\nu_{a,\mu}}{\nu_{a}}\right)^{1/2}\leq\sqrt{r_{n}}\left(\frac{q_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\right)^{1/2}.

Equation (B.56) shows thatmaxa⁡qa/νa=1+op​(1)\max_{a}q_{a}/\nu_{a}=1+o_{p}(1). Thereforemaxa∈A⁡|Ca|νa=Op​(rn).\max_{a\in A}\frac{|C_{a}|}{\nu_{a}}=O_{p}(\sqrt{r_{n}}).(B.57)

Step 3: control ofνa,μνa\frac{\nu_{a,\mu}}{\nu_{a}}.By definition (B.15) ofrnr_{n},maxa∈A⁡νa,μνa=rn.\max_{a\in A}\frac{\nu_{a,\mu}}{\nu_{a}}=r_{n}.(B.58)

Combining (B.56), (B.57), and
(B.58) givesmaxa∈A⁡|Qa−νaνa|=Op​(log⁡nLmin+log⁡nLmin+rn+rn),\max_{a\in A}\left|\frac{Q_{a}-\nu_{a}}{\nu_{a}}\right|=O_{p}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}}+\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}+\sqrt{r_{n}}+r_{n}\right),

AssumptionB.12implieslog⁡n/Lmin=o​(log⁡n/Lmin)\log n/L_{\min}=o(\sqrt{\log n/L_{\min}}), and
AssumptionB.11, together withbn→∞b_{n}\to\infty, impliesrn=o​(1)r_{n}=o(1)and hencern=O​(rn)r_{n}=O(\sqrt{r_{n}}). Thus the displayed bound onmaxa∈A⁡|(Qa−νa)/νa|\max_{a\in A}|(Q_{a}-\nu_{a})/\nu_{a}|gives the asserted rate forΔQ\Delta_{Q}.
AssumptionsB.11andB.12makelog⁡nLmin+rn→0,\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{L_{\min}}}+\sqrt{r_{n}}\to 0,

## Lemma 20.

Under AssumptionsB.1,B.7, andB.12,maxa=(I,h)∈Amaxt∈I(dt(h))2=Op(logn),maxa=(I,h)∈A1|I|∑t∈I(dt(h))4=Op(1).\max_{a=(I,h)\in A}\max_{t\in I}(d_{t}^{(h)})^{2}=O_{p}(\log n),\qquad\max_{a=(I,h)\in A}\frac{1}{|I|}\sum_{t\in I}(d_{t}^{(h)})^{4}=O_{p}(1).

## Proof.

The first claim follows from AssumptionB.1,
(B.2),n​H≤n​pnH\leq np, and
Proposition33, exactly as in
Lemma14. It remains to prove the fourth-moment bound.

SetXt(h)=(dt(h))4−𝔼​[(dt(h))4]X_{t}^{(h)}=(d_{t}^{(h)})^{4}-\mathbb{E}[(d_{t}^{(h)})^{4}]. Uniform
sub-Gaussianity gives a semiexponential tail with exponent1/21/2:supt,hℙ​(|Xt(h)|>u)≤C​exp⁡(−c​u),u>0.\sup_{t,h}\mathbb{P}(|X_{t}^{(h)}|>u)\leq C\exp(-c\sqrt{u}),\qquad u>0.

The fourth powers have uniformly bounded second moments and their quantile

Proposition32gives∫02​αX​(k)q​(u)2​𝑑u≲αX​(k)​log4⁡(e/αX​(k)).\int_{0}^{2\alpha_{X}(k)}q(u)^{2}\,du\lesssim\alpha_{X}(k)\log^{4}\!\bigl(e/\alpha_{X}(k)\bigr).

By AssumptionB.7,αX​(k)≤exp⁡(−K~2​kγmix)\alpha_{X}(k)\leq\exp(-\widetilde{K}_{2}k^{\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}}})for a
constantK~2>0\widetilde{K}_{2}>0. Hence∑k>0∫02​αX​(k)q​(u)2​𝑑u≲∑k>0e−K~2​kγmix​(1+kγmix)4<∞.\sum_{k>0}\int_{0}^{2\alpha_{X}(k)}q(u)^{2}\,du\lesssim\sum_{k>0}e^{-\widetilde{K}_{2}k^{\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}}}}(1+k^{\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}}})^{4}<\infty.

Together with the uniform boundedness of𝔼​[(Xt(h))2]\mathbb{E}[(X_{t}^{(h)})^{2}], equation (B.79) givesV=O​(1)V=O(1)uniformly overIIandhh. Takingy=C​my=Cmin
(B.78) givesℙ​(|∑t∈IXt(h)|>C​m)≤C​(m+1)​e−c​mγ4+C​e−c​m.\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\sum_{t\in I}X_{t}^{(h)}\right|>Cm\right)\leq C(m+1)e^{-cm^{\gamma_{4}}}+Ce^{-cm}.(B.59)

Because1/γ4=2+1/γmix1/\gamma_{4}=2+1/\gamma_{\mathrm{mix}}, AssumptionB.12implieslog⁡n=o​(Lminγ4)\log n=o(L_{\min}^{\gamma_{4}}). A union bound
over theppinterval-coordinate pairs in (B.59) proves

## B.4.4Proof of the centered Gaussian approximation

As in Lemma17, setZt,a∘:=n​1​{t∈I}​dt(h)|I|1/2−γ​νaγ.Z_{t,a}^{\circ}:=\frac{\sqrt{n}\,\mathbf{1}\{t\in I\}d_{t}^{(h)}}{|I|^{1/2-\gamma}\nu_{a}^{\gamma}}.(B.60)

Then𝕋∘=1n​∑t=1nZt∘.\mathbb{T}^{\circ}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}Z_{t}^{\circ}.

The sequence inherits the same geometric mixing rate, and AssumptionsB.8andB.3yieldc≤Var​(1n​∑t=1nZt,a∘)≤C∀a∈A.c\leq\mathrm{Var}\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}Z_{t,a}^{\circ}\right)\leq C\qquad\forall a\in A.

By AssumptionsB.1andB.2,‖Zt,a∘‖ψ2≤B0​(1∨σ−−1)​nLmin≤Bn,∀t,a.\|Z_{t,a}^{\circ}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq B_{0}(1\vee\sigma_{-}^{-1})\sqrt{\frac{n}{L_{\min}}}\leq B_{n},\qquad\forall t,a.(B.60a)

First, we assumep=Ω​(nκ)p=\Omega(n^{\kappa})for someκ>0\kappa>0.
Equation (B.60a) verifies the sub-Gaussian bound in
Proposition27; the variance bounds above verify its
nondegeneracy condition, and AssumptionB.7gives the required
geometric mixing. The first two rate conditions in
AssumptionB.10are exactly the CLT rate conditions in

## B.4.7Quadratic spectral kernel satisfies AssumptionB.9

## Lemma 21.

LetKQS​(x):={2512​π2​x2​{sin⁡(6​π​x/5)6​π​x/5−cos⁡(6​π​x/5)},x≠0,1,x=0.K_{\mathrm{QS}}(x):=\begin{cases}\dfrac{25}{12\pi^{2}x^{2}}\left\{\dfrac{\sin(6\pi x/5)}{6\pi x/5}-\cos(6\pi x/5)\right\},&x\neq 0,\\[4.30554pt]
1,&x=0.\end{cases}

ThenKQSK_{\mathrm{QS}}is symmetric and continuously differentiable onℝ\mathbb{R}, satisfiesKQS​(0)=1K_{\mathrm{QS}}(0)=1, and obeyssupx∈ℝ|KQS′​(x)|<∞,|KQS​(x)|≤CQS​(1+|x|)−2∀x∈ℝ\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|K_{\mathrm{QS}}^{\prime}(x)|<\infty,\qquad|K_{\mathrm{QS}}(x)|\leq C_{\mathrm{QS}}(1+|x|)^{-2}\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}

for some constantCQS<∞C_{\mathrm{QS}}<\infty. Consequently, AssumptionB.9holds for the quadratic spectral kernel withϑ=2\vartheta=2wheneverbn≍nρ,0<ρ<14.b_{n}\asymp n^{\rho},\qquad 0<\rho<\frac{1}{4}.

## Proof.

Symmetry is immediate becauseKQSK_{\mathrm{QS}}depends onxxonly throughx2x^{2}and the even functionssin⁡(z)/z\sin(z)/zandcos⁡(z)\cos(z). Forx≠0x\neq 0, the formula is smooth.
Setc:=6​π/5c:=6\pi/5. Using the Taylor expansionssin⁡(c​x)c​x=1−c2​x26+c4​x4120+O​(x6),cos⁡(c​x)=1−c2​x22+c4​x424+O​(x6),\frac{\sin(cx)}{cx}=1-\frac{c^{2}x^{2}}{6}+\frac{c^{4}x^{4}}{120}+O(x^{6}),\qquad\cos(cx)=1-\frac{c^{2}x^{2}}{2}+\frac{c^{4}x^{4}}{24}+O(x^{6}),

we obtainsin⁡(c​x)c​x−cos⁡(c​x)=c2​x23−c4​x430+O​(x6).\frac{\sin(cx)}{cx}-\cos(cx)=\frac{c^{2}x^{2}}{3}-\frac{c^{4}x^{4}}{30}+O(x^{6}).

Therefore,KQS​(x)=1−18​π2125​x2+O​(x4)(x→0).K_{\mathrm{QS}}(x)=1-\frac{18\pi^{2}}{125}x^{2}+O(x^{4})\qquad(x\to 0).

HenceKQSK_{\mathrm{QS}}extends continuously at0with value11, and the expansion also givesKQS′​(0)=0K_{\mathrm{QS}}^{\prime}(0)=0; thusKQS∈C1​(ℝ)K_{\mathrm{QS}}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}).

The positive-semidefiniteness condition follows from an explicit Fourier
representation. Withc=6​π/5c=6\pi/5, definefc​(u):=34​c​(1−u2c2)​𝟏​{|u|≤c}.f_{c}(u):=\frac{3}{4c}\left(1-\frac{u^{2}}{c^{2}}\right)\mathbf{1}\{|u|\leq c\}.

This is a nonnegative density, and direct integration givesKQS​(x)=∫−ccei​u​x​fc​(u)​𝑑u=3c2​x2​{sin⁡(c​x)c​x−cos⁡(c​x)}.K_{\mathrm{QS}}(x)=\int_{-c}^{c}e^{iux}f_{c}(u)\,du=\frac{3}{c^{2}x^{2}}\left\{\frac{\sin(cx)}{cx}-\cos(cx)\right\}.(B.73)

Becausefcf_{c}has compact support, differentiation under the integral is
valid and givesKQS′​(x)=∫−cci​u​ei​u​x​fc​(u)​𝑑u,supx∈ℝ|KQS′​(x)|≤∫−cc|u|​fc​(u)​𝑑u<∞.K_{\mathrm{QS}}^{\prime}(x)=\int_{-c}^{c}iue^{iux}f_{c}(u)\,du,\qquad\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|K_{\mathrm{QS}}^{\prime}(x)|\leq\int_{-c}^{c}|u|f_{c}(u)\,du<\infty.

Therefore, for arbitrary real numbersv1,…,vnv_{1},\ldots,v_{n},∑t,u=1nvt​vu​KQS​(t−ubn)=∫−cc|∑t=1nvt​ei​u​t/bn|2​fc​(u)​𝑑u≥0.\sum_{t,u=1}^{n}v_{t}v_{u}K_{\mathrm{QS}}\!\left(\frac{t-u}{b_{n}}\right)=\int_{-c}^{c}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{n}v_{t}e^{iut/b_{n}}\right|^{2}f_{c}(u)\,du\geq 0.

Thus every matrix in (B.52) is positive semidefinite.

For the tail bound, again withc=6​π/5c=6\pi/5, forx≠0x\neq 0we have|KQS​(x)|≤2512​π2​x2​(|sin⁡(c​x)c​x|+|cos⁡(c​x)|)≤2512​π2​x2​(1+1c​|x|).|K_{\mathrm{QS}}(x)|\leq\frac{25}{12\pi^{2}x^{2}}\left(\left|\frac{\sin(cx)}{cx}\right|+|\cos(cx)|\right)\leq\frac{25}{12\pi^{2}x^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{c|x|}\right).

In particular, for|x|≥1|x|\geq 1,|KQS​(x)|≤2512​π2​(1+1c)​|x|−2≤4​2512​π2​(1+1c)​(1+|x|)−2.|K_{\mathrm{QS}}(x)|\leq\frac{25}{12\pi^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{c}\right)|x|^{-2}\leq 4\frac{25}{12\pi^{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{c}\right)(1+|x|)^{-2}.

On the compact set[−1,1][-1,1], continuity impliessup|x|≤1|KQS​(x)|<∞\sup_{|x|\leq 1}|K_{\mathrm{QS}}(x)|<\infty. Enlarging the constant if necessary yields|KQS​(x)|≤CQS​(1+|x|)−2∀x∈ℝ.|K_{\mathrm{QS}}(x)|\leq C_{\mathrm{QS}}(1+|x|)^{-2}\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}.

Thus AssumptionB.9is satisfied withϑ=2\vartheta=2. The bandwidth restriction in that assumption then becomes0<ρ<ϑ−13​ϑ−2=14.0<\rho<\frac{\vartheta-1}{3\vartheta-2}=\frac{1}{4}.

This subsection shows that the test remains valid in Regime D with the
data-driven bandwidth used in the main text.
The implementation uses the quadratic spectral kernel ofAndrews (1991). For each horizonhh, it
fitsDt(h)=ch+ρh​Dt−1(h)+ϵh,t,D_{t}^{(h)}=c_{h}+\rho_{h}D_{t-1}^{(h)}+\epsilon_{h,t},

formsa^:=∑h=1H4​ρ^h2​σ^h4​(1−ρ^h)−8∑h=1Hσ^h4​(1−ρ^h)−4,b^n:=1.3221​(a^​n)1/5.\widehat{a}:=\frac{\sum_{h=1}^{H}4\widehat{\rho}_{h}^{2}\widehat{\sigma}_{h}^{4}(1-\widehat{\rho}_{h})^{-8}}{\sum_{h=1}^{H}\widehat{\sigma}_{h}^{4}(1-\widehat{\rho}_{h})^{-4}},\qquad\widehat{b}_{n}:=1.3221(\widehat{a}n)^{1/5}.(B.74)

The resulting multiplier covariance isΘ^n​(t,u):=KQS​(t−ub^n).\widehat{\Theta}_{n}(t,u):=K_{\mathrm{QS}}\!\left(\frac{t-u}{\widehat{b}_{n}}\right).

The next corollary shows that Theorem15continues to work for

convergence ofa^\widehat{a}.

constantϵ>0\epsilon>0,a^−a0=op​(n−1/5−ϵ),\widehat{a}-a_{0}=o_{p}\bigl(n^{-1/5-\epsilon}\bigr),

and therefore, with probability1−o​(1)1-o(1)over data,c​bn∘≤b^n≤C​bn∘.cb_{n}^{\circ}\leq\widehat{b}_{n}\leq Cb_{n}^{\circ}.

On the eventc​bn∘≤b^n≤C​bn∘cb_{n}^{\circ}\leq\widehat{b}_{n}\leq Cb_{n}^{\circ}, the mean value
theorem givesmax1≤t≤n​∑u=1n|KQS​(t−ub^n)−KQS​(t−ubn∘)|\displaystyle\max_{1\leq t\leq n}\sum_{u=1}^{n}\left|K_{\mathrm{QS}}\!\left(\frac{t-u}{\widehat{b}_{n}}\right)-K_{\mathrm{QS}}\!\left(\frac{t-u}{b_{n}^{\circ}}\right)\right|≤C​|1b^n−1bn∘|​∑m=1n−1m​(1+mbn∘)−2\displaystyle\leq C\left|\frac{1}{\widehat{b}_{n}}-\frac{1}{b_{n}^{\circ}}\right|\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}m\left(1+\frac{m}{b_{n}^{\circ}}\right)^{-2}≤C​|b^n−bn∘|​log⁡n=op​((log⁡n)−2),\displaystyle\leq C|\widehat{b}_{n}-b_{n}^{\circ}|\log n=o_{p}\bigl((\log n)^{-2}\bigr),

because|b^n−bn∘|​log⁡n=op​((log⁡n)−2)|\widehat{b}_{n}-b_{n}^{\circ}|\log n=o_{p}((\log n)^{-2}).

For any positive bandwidthbb, the Fourier representation

ThusΘ^n\widehat{\Theta}_{n}is a valid covariance matrix for the multipliers.

The row-sum argument in Lemma16gives, with probability1−o​(1)1-o(1)over data,maxt​∑u|Θ^n​(t,u)|≤C​b^n≤C​bn∘,maxt​∑uΘ^n​(t,u)2≤C​b^n≤C​bn∘.\max_{t}\sum_{u}|\widehat{\Theta}_{n}(t,u)|\leq C\widehat{b}_{n}\leq Cb_{n}^{\circ},\qquad\max_{t}\sum_{u}\widehat{\Theta}_{n}(t,u)^{2}\leq C\widehat{b}_{n}\leq Cb_{n}^{\circ}.(B.75)

It remains to verify the fixed-denominator covariance condition
(B.37) in
Lemma9; by (B.2), in
Regime D it is enough to show‖Σ^​(Θ^n)−Σ∘‖max=op​((log⁡n)−2)\|\widehat{\Sigma}(\widehat{\Theta}_{n})-\Sigma_{\circ}\|_{\max}=o_{p}((\log n)^{-2}).
For any covariance matrixΘ\Theta, defineΣ^a​b​(Θ):=∑t∈Ia∑u∈IbΘ​(t,u)​(Dt(ha)−D¯a)​(Du(hb)−D¯b)|Ia|1/2−γ​|Ib|1/2−γ​Qaγ​Qbγ.\widehat{\Sigma}_{ab}(\Theta):=\frac{\sum_{t\in I_{a}}\sum_{u\in I_{b}}\Theta(t,u)\left(D_{t}^{(h_{a})}-\bar{D}_{a}\right)\left(D_{u}^{(h_{b})}-\bar{D}_{b}\right)}{|I_{a}|^{1/2-\gamma}|I_{b}|^{1/2-\gamma}Q_{a}^{\gamma}Q_{b}^{\gamma}}.

ThenΣ^a​b​(Θ^n)−Σ^a​b​(Θn∘)=va⊤​(Θ^n−Θn∘)​vb.\widehat{\Sigma}_{ab}(\widehat{\Theta}_{n})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{ab}(\Theta_{n}^{\circ})=v_{a}^{\top}(\widehat{\Theta}_{n}-\Theta_{n}^{\circ})v_{b}.

Lemma19and AssumptionB.2implymaxa⁡‖va‖2=Op​(1)\max_{a}\left\lVert v_{a}\right\rVert_{2}=O_{p}(1). Hencemaxa,b∈A⁡|Σ^a​b​(Θ^n)−Σ^a​b​(Θn∘)|\displaystyle\max_{a,b\in A}\left|\widehat{\Sigma}_{ab}(\widehat{\Theta}_{n})-\widehat{\Sigma}_{ab}(\Theta_{n}^{\circ})\right|=maxa,b∈A⁡|va⊤​(Θ^n−Θn∘)​vb|\displaystyle=\max_{a,b\in A}\left|v_{a}^{\top}(\widehat{\Theta}_{n}-\Theta_{n}^{\circ})v_{b}\right|≤(maxa∈A⁡‖va‖2)2​‖Θ^n−Θn∘‖op\displaystyle\leq\left(\max_{a\in A}\left\lVert v_{a}\right\rVert_{2}\right)^{2}\left\lVert\widehat{\Theta}_{n}-\Theta_{n}^{\circ}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}=Op​(1)​op​((log⁡n)−2)=op​((log⁡n)−2).\displaystyle=O_{p}(1)o_{p}\bigl((\log n)^{-2}\bigr)=o_{p}\bigl((\log n)^{-2}\bigr).

The proof in SectionB.4.5applied with the deterministic benchmarkΘn∘\Theta_{n}^{\circ}gives‖Σ^​(Θn∘)−Σ∘‖max=op​((log⁡n)−2).\left\lVert\widehat{\Sigma}(\Theta_{n}^{\circ})-\Sigma_{\circ}\right\rVert_{\max}=o_{p}\bigl((\log n)^{-2}\bigr).

## B.5Miscellany

This section states the key technical results our proof relies on for completeness.

The next proposition is the independent high-dimensional Gaussian
approximation ofChernozhukovet al.(2017, Theorem 2.1 and Proposition 2.1).

## Proposition 23.

LetX1,…,XnX_{1},\dots,X_{n}be independent centered random vectors inℝp\mathbb{R}^{p}, and letSn:=1n​∑i=1nXi,Σ:=Cov​(Sn),G∼N​(0,Σ).S_{n}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i},\qquad\Sigma:=\mathrm{Cov}(S_{n}),\qquad G\sim N(0,\Sigma).

Assume that, for some constantsb>0b>0andBn≥1B_{n}\geq 1,min1≤j≤p⁡Σj​j≥b,max1≤i≤n,1≤j≤p⁡‖Xi​j‖ψ1≤Bn.\min_{1\leq j\leq p}\Sigma_{jj}\geq b,\qquad\max_{1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq j\leq p}\|X_{ij}\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq B_{n}.

Then there exists a constantC=C​(b)<∞C=C(b)<\inftysuch thatsupA∈ℛp|ℙ​(Sn∈A)−ℙ​(G∈A)|≤C​(Bn2​log7⁡(p​n)n)1/6,\sup_{A\in\mathcal{R}^{p}}\bigl|\mathbb{P}(S_{n}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(G\in A)\bigr|\leq C\left(\frac{B_{n}^{2}\log^{7}(pn)}{n}\right)^{1/6},

whereℛp\mathcal{R}^{p}denotes the class of coordinatewise hyperrectangles inℝp\mathbb{R}^{p}. In particular, ifBn2​log7⁡(p​n)=o​(n)B_{n}^{2}\log^{7}(pn)=o(n), then the left-hand side converges to0.

The next proposition is the Gaussian anti-concentration bound ofChernozhukovet al.(2015, Theorem 3 and Corollary 1).

## Proposition 24.

LetG=(G1,…,Gp)⊤G=(G_{1},\dots,G_{p})^{\top}be a centered Gaussian vector such that0<σ¯≤Var​(Gj)≤σ¯<∞∀j∈[p].0<\underline{\sigma}\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(G_{j})}\leq\bar{\sigma}<\infty\qquad\forall j\in[p].

Then there exists a constantC<∞C<\infty, depending only onσ¯\underline{\sigma}andσ¯\bar{\sigma}, such that for everyε>0\varepsilon>0,supx∈ℝℙ​(|max1≤j≤p⁡Gj−x|≤ε)≤C​ε​{1∨log⁡(p/ε)}.\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}\left(|\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}-x|\leq\varepsilon\right)\leq C\varepsilon\left\{1\vee\sqrt{\log(p/\varepsilon)}\right\}.

In particular, ifεn↓0\varepsilon_{n}\downarrow 0andεn​1∨log⁡(p/εn)→0\varepsilon_{n}\sqrt{1\vee\log(p/\varepsilon_{n})}\to 0, thensupx∈ℝℙ​(|max1≤j≤p⁡Gj−x|≤εn)→0.\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}\left(|\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}-x|\leq\varepsilon_{n}\right)\to 0.

The same conclusion holds withmax1≤j≤p⁡|Gj|\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|G_{j}|in place ofmax1≤j≤p⁡Gj\max_{1\leq j\leq p}G_{j}after replacingppby2​p2p.

The next proposition is the Gaussian comparison inequality fromChernozhukovet al.(2015, Theorem 2).

## Proposition 25.

LetX=(X1,…,Xp)⊤X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{p})^{\top}andY=(Y1,…,Yp)⊤Y=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{p})^{\top}be centered Gaussian vectors with covariance matricesΣX\Sigma^{X}andΣY\Sigma^{Y}. Assume that0<σ¯≤Var​(Xj),Var​(Yj)≤σ¯<∞∀j∈[p],0<\underline{\sigma}\leq\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(X_{j})},\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(Y_{j})}\leq\bar{\sigma}<\infty\qquad\forall j\in[p],

and defineΔ:=‖ΣX−ΣY‖max.\Delta:=\|\Sigma^{X}-\Sigma^{Y}\|_{\max}.

Then there exists a constantC<∞C<\infty, depending only onσ¯\underline{\sigma}andσ¯\bar{\sigma}, such thatsupx∈ℝ|ℙ​(max1≤j≤p⁡Xj≤x)−ℙ​(max1≤j≤p⁡Yj≤x)|≤C​Δ1/3​{1∨log⁡(p/Δ)}2/3,\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq p}X_{j}\leq x\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq p}Y_{j}\leq x\right)\right|\leq C\Delta^{1/3}\bigl\{1\vee\log(p/\Delta)\bigr\}^{2/3},

with the convention that the right-hand side equals0whenΔ=0\Delta=0.
In particular, ifΔn=o​((log⁡p)−2)\Delta_{n}=o((\log p)^{-2}), then the Kolmogorov distance between the two Gaussian maxima converges to0.

## Proposition 30.

LetX1,…,XnX_{1},\dots,X_{n}be centered random vectors inℝp\mathbb{R}^{p}, and defineSn,x:=1n​∑t=1nXt,Ξ:=Cov​(Sn,x).S_{n,x}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}X_{t},\qquad\Xi:=\mathrm{Cov}(S_{n,x}).

For a class𝒜\mathcal{A}of Borel subsets ofℝp\mathbb{R}^{p}, defineρn(𝒜):=supA∈𝒜|ℙ(Sn,x∈A)−ℙ(G∈A)|,ρ^n(𝒜):=supA∈𝒜|ℙ(Sn,x∈A)−ℙ(G^∈A∣X1,…,Xn)|.\rho_{n}(\mathcal{A}):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl|\mathbb{P}(S_{n,x}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(G\in A)\bigr|,\quad\widehat{\rho}_{n}(\mathcal{A}):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\bigl|\mathbb{P}(S_{n,x}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(\widehat{G}\in A\mid X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\bigr|.

Let𝒜re\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{re}}denote the class of all hyper-rectangles inℝp\mathbb{R}^{p}, namely all sets of the formA=∏j=1p[aj,bj],−∞≤aj≤bj≤∞.A=\prod_{j=1}^{p}[a_{j},b_{j}],\qquad-\infty\leq a_{j}\leq b_{j}\leq\infty.

These sets include one-sided rectangles by allowing infinite endpoints.
For eachj∈[p]j\in[p], letVn,j:=Var​(1n​∑t=1nXt,j).V_{n,j}:=\mathrm{Var}\!\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}X_{t,j}\right).

Assume that there exists a constantK3>0K_{3}>0such thatminj∈[p]⁡Vn,j≥K3\min_{j\in[p]}V_{n,j}\geq K_{3}.
Further, assume thatp≥nκp\geq n^{\kappa}for some constantκ>0\kappa>0. Thenρ^n​(𝒜re)≲ρn​(𝒜re)+‖Ξ^n−Ξ‖max1/3​(log⁡p)2/3,\widehat{\rho}_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{re}})\lesssim\rho_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{re}})+\left\lVert\widehat{\Xi}_{n}-\Xi\right\rVert_{\max}^{1/3}(\log p)^{2/3},

where≲\lesssimhides a universal positive constant.

## Proposition 31.

Let(Xj)j≥1(X_{j})_{j\geq 1}be centered real-valued random variables, and define their strong-mixing coefficients byαX​(ℓ):=supk≥1supA∈σ​(X1,…,Xk),B∈σ​(Xk+ℓ,Xk+ℓ+1,…)|ℙ​(A∩B)−ℙ​(A)​ℙ​(B)|,ℓ≥1.\alpha_{X}(\ell):=\sup_{k\geq 1}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\in\sigma(X_{1},\dots,X_{k}),\\
B\in\sigma(X_{k+\ell},X_{k+\ell+1},\dots)\end{subarray}}\big|\mathbb{P}(A\cap B)-\mathbb{P}(A)\mathbb{P}(B)\big|,\qquad\ell\geq 1.

Assume that there exist constantsc>0c>0,b>0b>0,γ1>0\gamma_{1}>0, andγ2∈(0,∞]\gamma_{2}\in(0,\infty]such thatαX​(n)≤exp⁡(−c​nγ1)for all​n≥1,\alpha_{X}(n)\leq\exp\!\bigl(-cn^{\gamma_{1}}\bigr)\qquad\text{for all }n\geq 1,

andsupj≥1ℙ​(|Xj|>t)≤exp⁡(1−(t/b)γ2)for all​t>0.\sup_{j\geq 1}\mathbb{P}(|X_{j}|>t)\leq\exp\!\Bigl(1-(t/b)^{\gamma_{2}}\Bigr)\qquad\text{for all }t>0.

Letγ:=(1γ1+1γ2)−1,φM​(x):=(x∧M)∨(−M),\gamma:=\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}\right)^{-1},\qquad\varphi_{M}(x):=(x\wedge M)\vee(-M),

and assumeγ<1\gamma<1. DefineV:=supM>0supi≥1(Var​(φM​(Xi))+2​∑j>i|Cov​(φM​(Xi),φM​(Xj))|).V:=\sup_{M>0}\sup_{i\geq 1}\left(\mathrm{Var}\bigl(\varphi_{M}(X_{i})\bigr)+2\sum_{j>i}\bigl|\mathrm{Cov}\bigl(\varphi_{M}(X_{i}),\varphi_{M}(X_{j})\bigr)\bigr|\right).(B.77)

Then there exist constantsC0,C1,C2,η0∈(0,∞)C_{0},C_{1},C_{2},\eta_{0}\in(0,\infty), depending only on(b,c,γ1,γ2)(b,c,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}), such that for everyn≥4n\geq 4and everyy≥C0​(log⁡n)η0y\geq C_{0}(\log n)^{\eta_{0}},ℙ​(max1≤j≤n⁡|∑r=1jXr|≥y)≤(n+1)​exp⁡(−yγC1)+exp⁡(−y2C2+C2​n​V).\mathbb{P}\!\left(\max_{1\leq j\leq n}\left|\sum_{r=1}^{j}X_{r}\right|\geq y\right)\leq(n+1)\exp\!\left(-\frac{y^{\gamma}}{C_{1}}\right)+\exp\!\left(-\frac{y^{2}}{C_{2}+C_{2}nV}\right).(B.78)

## Proposition 32.

Consider the setting of Proposition31. Foru∈(0,1]u\in(0,1], define the marginal quantilesq|Xi|​(u):=inf{t>0:ℙ​(|Xi|>t)≤u},q​(u):=supi≥1q|Xi|​(u),q_{|X_{i}|}(u):=\inf\{t>0:\mathbb{P}(|X_{i}|>t)\leq u\},\qquad q(u):=\sup_{i\geq 1}q_{|X_{i}|}(u),

Then the strong-mixing covariance inequality(Merlevèdeet al.,2011, see, e.g., Remark 3)impliesV≤supi≥1𝔼​[Xi2]+4​∑k>0∫02​αX​(k)q​(u)2​𝑑u.V\leq\sup_{i\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{2}]+4\sum_{k>0}\int_{0}^{2\alpha_{X}(k)}q(u)^{2}\,du.(B.79)

## Orliczψ2\psi_{2}andψ1\psi_{1}norms.

FollowingVershynin (2018, Definitions 2.5.6 and 2.7.5), define for a real-valued random variableXX‖X‖ψ2:=inf{t>0:𝔼​exp⁡(X2/t2)≤2},‖X‖ψ1:=inf{t>0:𝔼​exp⁡(|X|/t)≤2}.\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}:=\inf\bigl\{t>0:\ \mathbb{E}\exp(X^{2}/t^{2})\leq 2\bigr\},\qquad\|X\|_{\psi_{1}}:=\inf\bigl\{t>0:\ \mathbb{E}\exp(|X|/t)\leq 2\bigr\}.

We callXXsub-Gaussianif‖X‖ψ2<∞\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}<\inftyandsub-exponentialif‖X‖ψ1<∞\|X\|_{\psi_{1}}<\infty.
ByVershynin (2018, Proposition 2.5.2), theψ2\psi_{2}norm is equivalent to the standard sub-Gaussian tail and moment conditions; byVershynin (2018, Proposition 2.7.1), theψ1\psi_{1}norm is equivalent to the standard sub-exponential tail and moment conditions.

The next proposition gives maximal bounds for finitely many sub-Gaussian
variables, followingVershynin (2018, Proposition 2.5.2 and Exercise 2.5.10).

## Proposition 33.

LetX1,…,XmX_{1},\dots,X_{m}be real-valued random variables satisfyingmax1≤j≤m⁡‖Xj‖ψ2≤K.\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|X_{j}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K.

Then there exist universal constantsC,c∈(0,∞)C,c\in(0,\infty)such that𝔼​[max1≤j≤m⁡|Xj|]≤C​K​1∨log⁡m\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|X_{j}|\Bigr]\leq CK\sqrt{1\vee\log m}

and, for everyu≥0u\geq 0,ℙ​(max1≤j≤m⁡|Xj|>C​K​(log⁡(2​m)+u))≤2​e−c​u2.\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|X_{j}|>CK\bigl(\sqrt{\log(2m)}+u\bigr)\Bigr)\leq 2e^{-cu^{2}}.

In particular,max1≤j≤m⁡|Xj|=Op​(K​1∨log⁡m).\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|X_{j}|=O_{p}\bigl(K\sqrt{1\vee\log m}\bigr).

No independence is required.

The next proposition gives the standard sub-Gaussian bound for weighted sums
of independent centered variablesX1,…,XmX_{1},\dots,X_{m}, followingVershynin (2018, Proposition 2.6.1).

## Proposition 34.

LetX1,…,XmX_{1},\dots,X_{m}be independent centered real-valued random variables, and setKj:=‖Xj‖ψ2,V2:=(∑j=1mKj2)1/2.K_{j}:=\left\lVert X_{j}\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}},\qquad V_{2}:=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}K_{j}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Then there exists a universal constantC<∞C<\inftysuch that‖∑j=1mXj‖ψ2≤C​V2.\Bigl\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}X_{j}\Bigr\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq CV_{2}.

More generally, for deterministic weightsa1,…,am∈ℝa_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in\mathbb{R},‖∑j=1maj​Xj‖ψ2≤C​(∑j=1maj2​Kj2)1/2.\Bigl\|\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}X_{j}\Bigr\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}^{2}K_{j}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

The next proposition gives the standard product rule turning sub-Gaussian
products into sub-exponential variables, followingVershynin (2018, Lemma 2.7.7).

## Proposition 35.

LetXXandYYbe sub-gaussian random variables. ThenX​YXYis sub-exponential. Moreover,‖X​Y‖ψ1≤‖X‖ψ2​‖Y‖ψ2.\|XY\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}.

The next proposition gives maximal bounds for finitely many sub-exponential
variables, followingVershynin (2018, Proposition 2.7.1).

## Proposition 36.

LetY1,…,YmY_{1},\dots,Y_{m}be real-valued random variables satisfyingmax1≤j≤m⁡‖Yj‖ψ1≤K.\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|Y_{j}\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq K.

Then there exist universal constantsC,c∈(0,∞)C,c\in(0,\infty)such that𝔼​[max1≤j≤m⁡|Yj|]≤C​K​(1∨log⁡m)\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|Y_{j}|\Bigr]\leq CK(1\vee\log m)

and, for everyu≥0u\geq 0,ℙ​(max1≤j≤m⁡|Yj|>C​K​(log⁡(2​m)+u))≤2​e−c​u.\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|Y_{j}|>CK\bigl(\log(2m)+u\bigr)\Bigr)\leq 2e^{-cu}.

In particular,max1≤j≤m⁡|Yj|=Op​(K​(1∨log⁡m)).\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|Y_{j}|=O_{p}\bigl(K(1\vee\log m)\bigr).

Again, no independence is required.

## Appendix CSimulation Results for Partially Studentized Scan Tests

Section3of the main draft develops the scan test as a one-sided procedure for
detecting forecast outperformance that may occur only over unknown target-month
intervals and unknown horizons. The theory justifies the bootstrap scan test
asymptotically, but the empirical application has onlyn=215n=215target months
and uses short interval collections for expert selection. This appendix
therefore documents the finite-sample calibration exercises behind the
implementation choices in Section3. The first part calibrates the
partial-studentization exponentγ\gammafor the horizon-by-horizon scan tests.
The second part repeats the calibration for the horizon-pooled pre/post-COVID
use of the scan test. The final part benchmarks these choices against the
closely related Giacomini–Rossi fluctuation test ofGiacomini and Rossi (2010).

## C.1Simulation and the choice ofγ\gamma

The scan statistic in Section3maximizes a partially studentized statistic
over many intervals and horizons. This maximization is what lets the test find
localized outperformance without specifying its timing or forecast horizon in
advance, but it also creates the main finite-sample concern: aggressive
studentization can make short, noisy intervals look too extreme. We therefore
chooseγ\gammaby simulating the null in a way that preserves the empirical
scale, cross-horizon dependence, and date-specific heterogeneity of the observed
loss-differential panels.

The simulation setting is under the null. It fixes an empirical
loss-differential panelDt(h)D_{t}^{(h)}and drawsDt,k(h)=ξt,k​Dt(h),ξt,k=sign⁡(Zt,k),D_{t,k}^{(h)}=\xi_{t,k}D_{t}^{(h)},\qquad\xi_{t,k}=\operatorname{sign}(Z_{t,k}),

whereZt,kZ_{t,k}is a stationary Gaussian AR(1) process with autocorrelationρ\rho. To chooseρ\rho, we fit an AR(1) model to each comparison
(sarima–micro, macro–micro, and sarima–macro) and each horizonh=1,…,24h=1,\ldots,24. The box
plots of the estimates are given below. Thus, we chooseρ∈{−0.25,0,0.25,0.5}\rho\in\{-0.25,0,0.25,0.5\}in our simulations.Figure C.1:AR(1) coefficient estimates across horizons for sarima–micro, macro–micro, and sarima–macro.

## C.1.1Simulation results for occasional outperformance

Occasional outperformance is the expert-selection use emphasized in Section3:
the candidate forecast should enter the pool if it improves on the benchmark
over some unknown short subperiod and horizon. We use the interval lengths{6,7,…,12}\{6,7,\ldots,12\}to target half-year to one-year episodes, where a forecast
can be useful during a localized inflation episode even if it is not uniformly
better over the full sample. This is also the most demanding case for size,
because the scan searches over many short windows.

## C.1.2Simulation results for overall outperformance

Overall outperformance is the companion expert-selection diagnostic from
Section3. Instead of searching for a localized episode, it fixes the interval
to the full balanced sample and asks whether one forecast improves on another
over the sample as a whole at some horizon. This removes the interval-search
noise from the occasional scan and can be more stable when predictive gains are
sustained rather than concentrated in a short spell.

## C.1.3Choice ofγ\gamma

This subsection converts the two calibration exercises into the tuning rule
used in the empirical scan checks. Because Section3allows either Regime I or
Regime D depending on serial dependence, the decision must control size not only
under each fixed multiplier regime but also after the Ljung–Box pre-test chooses
between them.

The simulation results suggestγ=0.1\gamma=0.1avoids over-rejections across the
board. So we chooseγ=0.1\gamma=0.1for the scan test. A few rejection
probabilities are slightly above the nominal level whenρ=0.5\rho=0.5, but the
inflation is minor and occurs under the strongest positive serial dependence
considered in the simulation grid. We will check robustness by also reporting the
empirical results for the adjacent choicesγ=0.05\gamma=0.05andγ=0.15\gamma=0.15.

To confirm that the pre-test does not inflate size, we run the same simulation
on the scan test with the Ljung–Box pre-test and the above chosenγ\gammafor
each comparison. We report the results for both the occasional- and
overall-outperformance interval sets.Table C.7:Simulation for the Ljung–Box pre-test scan test withδ=0.05\delta=0.05andγ=0.1\gamma=0.1under both multiplier regimes, using occasional-outperformance lengths{6,7,…,12}\{6,7,\ldots,12\}. The simulations above motivate the baseline choiceγ=0.1\gamma=0.1by focusing on
size control under null designs calibrated to the observed loss-differential
panels. The empirical conclusion should not, however, hinge on this single
tuning value. We therefore report the actual scan-test p-values for the two

valuesγ=0.05\gamma=0.05andγ=0.15\gamma=0.15. The occasional-outperformance metric

candidate forecast improves on the benchmark during some localized episode and
horizon. The overall-outperformance metric uses the full balanced sample

as a whole at some horizon. Reporting both metrics across nearby values ofγ\gammachecks that the substantive expert-selection evidence is robust to the

## C.2Simulations for the horizon-pooled scan test

Section3also describes a complementary use of the scan test for a
pre-specified calendar window, while pooling across horizons. This question is
different from expert selection: the window, such as the pre-COVID or
post-COVID period, is fixed in advance, and the test asks whether the average
loss differential across horizons is positive within that window. Because the
statistic is now built from the horizon-pooled seriesD¯t\bar{D}_{t}rather than
from the full interval-horizon panel, we run a separate null calibration for
this version of the procedure.

For each empirical panel, we
fitD¯t=c+ρ​D¯t−1+ut\bar{D}_{t}=c+\rho\bar{D}_{t-1}+u_{t}to the horizon-pooled series. The
estimated AR(1) coefficients are reported in TableC.12.Table C.12:AR(1) estimates for the horizon-pooled loss-differential series.Comparisonρ^\hat{\rho}95% confidence interval(sarima+macro)−three experts(\text{sarima}+\text{macro})-\text{three experts}0.050[−0.086,0.187][-0.086,0.187]sarima–micro0.185[0.052,0.317][0.052,0.317]sarima–macro0.160[0.027,0.293][0.027,0.293]

The null DGP is
the same sign-AR(1) construction described above, applied to the horizon-pooled
series. We runγ∈{0,0.05,0.1,…,0.5}\gamma\in\{0,0.05,0.1,\ldots,0.5\}, 1000 Monte Carlo samples,
and 999 bootstrap draws. Panels (a) and (b) fix Regime I and Regime D,
respectively; panel (c) applies the Ljung–Box pre-test withδ=0.05\delta=0.05.
For the horizon-pooled calibration tables below, we simulate onlyρ∈{0,0.15,0.3}\rho\in\{0,0.15,0.3\}.

## C.3Giacomini–Rossi fluctuation test

Section3discusses theGiacomini and Rossi (2010)fluctuation test as the
closest classical comparison: it also looks for forecast-instability episodes,
but it uses fixed window lengths and fixed horizons rather than a joint scan
over unknown intervals and horizons. We include it here to make clear what
changes when the localized-outperformance problem is treated with the classical
Brownian-limit calibration instead of the one-sided multiplier-bootstrap scan
used in the main procedure.

For comparison, we implement a one-sided version of this fluctuation test. For
a fixed horizonhhand a fixed window lengthmm, defineGm(h)=max1≤s≤n−m+1⁡∑t=ss+m−1Dt(h)m​σ^h2,G_{m}^{(h)}=\max_{1\leq s\leq n-m+1}\frac{\sum_{t=s}^{s+m-1}D_{t}^{(h)}}{\sqrt{m\widehat{\sigma}_{h}^{2}}},

whereσ^h2\widehat{\sigma}_{h}^{2}is a full-sample Newey–West long-run variance
estimate for the loss differential at horizonhh. We use lagh−1h-1in this
estimate. The alternative is one-sided: large positive values indicate that
the first forecast has larger loss than the second forecast over some
fixed-length window.

Under the local equal-predictive-ability null and a functional central limit
theorem,maxs⁡∑t=ss+m−1Dt(h)m​σ^h2⇒supsW​(s+m/n)−W​(s)m/n,\max_{s}\frac{\sum_{t=s}^{s+m-1}D_{t}^{(h)}}{\sqrt{m\widehat{\sigma}_{h}^{2}}}\Rightarrow\sup_{s}\frac{W(s+m/n)-W(s)}{\sqrt{m/n}},

whereWWis standard Brownian motion. In the simulations below we approximate
this null distribution on the observed grid by simulating iid standard-normal
increments and taking the maximum rolling sum divided bym\sqrt{m}.

We run the same sign-AR(1) null simulation described above withm∈{6,7,…,12}m\in\{6,7,\ldots,12\}, 1000 Monte Carlo samples, and 200000 Brownian-limit
draws for each value ofmm. The test is horizon-by-horizon, so each table
reports the mean rejection probability across the 24 horizons. This mirrors
the occasional-outperformance calibration while respecting the fixed-window,
fixed-horizon structure of the Giacomini–Rossi statistic.Table C.18:Giacomini–Rossi simulation for sarima–micro. ## Appendix FRobustness: Alternative Encodings, Aggregations, and Data Filtering

The baseline micro encoding used in the main text retains 11 features per
COICOP1 category (fraction of zero price changes, nine deciles of
the non-zero price-change distribution, and its mean), aggregates within
each category using CPI weights, and uses the full sales-included price
quotes, for a total of11×11=12111\times 11=121statistics per month. This
appendix documents four robustness checks against this baseline.
AppendixF.1replaces the baseline encoding with
the full augmented encoding (29 features per category,11×29=31911\times 29=319statistics per month) while keeping CPI-weighted aggregation and
sales-included quotes. AppendixF.2keeps the
11-feature encoding but replaces CPI-weighted aggregation with
equal-weighted (unweighted) aggregation across products within each COICOP1
category. AppendixF.3keeps the 11-feature encoding and
CPI-weighted aggregation but excludes ONS-sale-flagged price quotes from
the underlying micro panel. AppendixF.4likewise keeps the
encoding and aggregation but replaces temporary sale prices with the regular
price inferred by theNakamura and Steinsson (2008)filter (parameterisation
A) before computing the per-category statistics. Each appendix reports the consequences of the
alternative choice at the
standalone micro stage and inside the three-expert online-learning
combination.

## F.1Augmented Encoding vs Baseline

The full augmented encoding, as described in
Subsection4.1, adds the variance (with Bessel’s
correction), the standardised third
and fifth central moments and the excess kurtosis of the non-zero price-change
distribution, the nine deciles of the time-since-last-change distribution, and
the five moments of the same distribution, raising the per-category feature
count from 11 to 29 and the per-month feature count from 121 to 319. This is
the same set of micro variables as the six-group Shapley decomposition
(AppendixG.4), which spans exactly this augmented
feature set; the decomposition’s coalition refits reuse the baseline expert’s
frozen tuning, whereas the augmented expert here is tuned on its own
cached real-time schedule.

## F.1.1Standalone Micro Forecast

TableF.1reports the
percentage MAE improvement of the standalone micro forecast relative to the univariate forecast
under both encodings, for the pre-2020 (2015–2019) and post-2020
(2020–2024) windows and all horizonsh=1,…,24h=1,\ldots,24. Averaging within
four horizon groups (h=1h=1to66,77to1212,1313to1818, and1919to2424), the augmented encoding’s percentage MAE improvements relative to
the univariate forecast are−35.1%-35.1\%,−25.6%-25.6\%,−20.6%-20.6\%, and−27.1%-27.1\%in the
pre-2020 window and−0.4%-0.4\%,5.4%5.4\%,−3.4%-3.4\%, and22.6%22.6\%in the
post-2020 window. The corresponding gaps relative to the baseline
encoding (augmented minus baseline) are−5.7-5.7,−6.8-6.8,−3.3-3.3, and+5.5+5.5percentage points pre-2020 (averaging a2.52.5percentage-point
cost) and−9.0-9.0,−5.4-5.4,−11.9-11.9, and−2.7-2.7percentage points post-2020
(averaging a7.37.3percentage-point cost). The augmented encoding
therefore underperforms the baseline at the standalone stage in both
windows, with the cost now concentrated in the post-COVID period, where
the added higher-moment and time-since blocks are noisiest, at short and
medium-long horizons. This is consistent with overfitting risk rising
in the number of predictors when the univariate forecast itself remains competitive, as
discussed in Subsection4.1.Table F.1:Micro Forecast Percentage MAE Improvement Relative to the Univariate Forecast: Baseline versus Augmented EncodingPre-2020Post-2020hhBaselineAugmentedBaselineAugmented1-35.67-49.218.4914.852-28.92-51.744.46-3.263-29.10-37.632.141.114-30.97-28.429.67-7.855-32.52-17.426.73-8.236-19.51-26.0520.310.707-17.33-19.3313.00-0.448-17.05-25.528.638.039-27.49-39.0714.638.3310-18.19-21.7310.438.8411-19.84-24.4216.0410.1412-12.81-23.511.79-2.6713-10.32-7.6912.95-0.4914-0.13-13.6115.28-3.3015-22.62-28.9414.270.5516-21.06-23.2711.03-10.6517-31.47-26.04-3.33-3.2818-18.48-23.920.82-3.1519-28.04-17.379.074.7820-26.65-35.149.3410.5621-32.13-32.6635.4231.0122-44.26-33.7632.2731.4323-36.87-28.2639.3335.8124-27.88-15.4526.4621.85

Notes:Each cell reports the percentage MAE improvement of the standalone micro forecast relative to the univariate forecast at horizonhh. The baseline uses the 11-feature encoding; the augmented robustness check uses the 29-feature encoding (adding the higher moments of the price-change distribution and the time-since-last-change block), the same feature universe as the six-group Shapley decomposition.
Positive values indicate lower MAE than the univariate forecast. Evaluation
windows are 2015–2019 and 2020–2024.

## F.1.2Three-Expert Online Learning

TableF.2reports
the percentage MAE improvement of the three-expert patient Fixed Share

relative to the univariate forecast, with the micro model trained on the baseline and
augmented encodings respectively, holding the macro and univariate forecasts fixed.
Averaging within the same four horizon groups, the augmented-encoding
three-expert combination’s percentage MAE improvements are0.8%0.8\%,−2.5%-2.5\%,−2.8%-2.8\%, and−1.7%-1.7\%in the pre-2020 window and8.0%8.0\%,12.1%12.1\%,8.1%8.1\%, and23.3%23.3\%in the post-2020 window. The corresponding gaps
relative to the baseline-encoding three-expert combination are−0.4-0.4,−1.1-1.1,−1.1-1.1, and+1.3+1.3percentage points pre-2020 (averaging a0.30.3percentage-point cost) and−4.0-4.0,−1.6-1.6,−3.8-3.8, and−1.9-1.9percentage points post-2020 (averaging a2.82.8percentage-point cost).

points once the augmented micro forecast is combined with the macro

## F.2Unweighted Aggregation vs Baseline

The unweighted aggregation keeps the 11-feature baseline encoding but
constructs each per-category statistic as an equal-weighted average
across products rather than weighting by CPI shares. This isolates the
contribution of the CPI-share weighting choice in the baseline.

## F.2.1Standalone Micro Forecast

TableF.3reports the
percentage MAE improvement of the standalone micro forecast relative to the univariate forecast
under CPI-weighted (baseline) and unweighted aggregation, for all
horizonsh=1,…,24h=1,\ldots,24. Averaging within four horizon groups
(h=1h=1to66,77to1212,1313to1818, and1919to2424), the
unweighted aggregation’s percentage MAE improvements relative to the univariate forecast
are−43.1%-43.1\%,−22.4%-22.4\%,−15.2%-15.2\%, and−23.9%-23.9\%in the pre-2020
window and−1.9%-1.9\%,7.2%7.2\%,8.4%8.4\%, and26.4%26.4\%in the post-2020
window. The corresponding gaps relative to the baseline (unweighted
minus baseline) are−13.6-13.6,−3.6-3.6,+2.1+2.1, and+8.7+8.7percentage points
pre-2020 (averaging a1.61.6percentage-point cost) and−10.6-10.6,−3.5-3.5,−0.1-0.1, and+1.1+1.1percentage points post-2020 (averaging a3.33.3percentage-point cost). Unweighted aggregation therefore underperforms
CPI-weighted aggregation on average in both windows, with the cost
concentrated at short horizons, where CPI shares concentrate on the most
volatile categories, and a partial offset at the longest horizons.Table F.3:Micro Forecast Percentage MAE Improvement Relative to the Univariate Forecast: Baseline versus Equal-Weighted AggregationPre-2020Post-2020hhBaselineUnweightedBaselineUnweighted1-35.67-62.028.49-1.742-28.92-58.464.46-2.483-29.10-39.902.14-0.984-30.97-34.319.67-8.295-32.52-28.486.73-1.426-19.51-35.1420.313.397-17.33-27.2113.0010.158-17.05-20.858.637.059-27.49-27.8014.6314.6210-18.19-15.6510.434.1311-19.84-26.4416.0410.3312-12.81-16.231.79-2.9113-10.32-6.3512.954.7114-0.13-1.5615.2811.3715-22.62-12.7214.278.4016-21.06-24.6011.039.7817-31.47-27.43-3.338.9718-18.48-18.750.827.3719-28.04-14.369.0712.1620-26.65-22.729.3411.2121-32.13-38.0535.4235.1222-44.26-25.2632.2731.5523-36.87-24.4839.3338.2224-27.88-18.6126.4630.44

## F.3Sales-Excluded Micro vs Baseline

The sales-excluded variant keeps the 11-feature encoding and CPI-weighted
aggregation but drops ONS-sale-flagged price quotes from the underlying
micro panel before computing the per-category statistics. This isolates
the contribution of temporary-sale price changes to the baseline forecast.

## F.3.1Standalone Micro Forecast

TableF.5reports the
percentage MAE improvement of the standalone micro forecast relative to the univariate forecast
under sales-included (baseline) and sales-excluded micro input, for all
horizonsh=1,…,24h=1,\ldots,24. Averaging within four horizon groups (h=1h=1to66,77to1212,1313to1818, and1919to2424), the sales-excluded
variant’s percentage MAE improvements relative to the univariate forecast are−43.6%-43.6\%,−18.2%-18.2\%,−16.8%-16.8\%, and−10.4%-10.4\%in the pre-2020 window and−10.3%-10.3\%,−1.7%-1.7\%,7.3%7.3\%, and22.5%22.5\%in the post-2020 window. The
corresponding gaps relative to the baseline (sales-excluded minus
baseline) are−14.1-14.1,+0.6+0.6,+0.6+0.6, and+22.2+22.2percentage points
pre-2020 (averaging a2.32.3percentage-point gain) and−19.0-19.0,−12.5-12.5,−1.2-1.2, and−2.8-2.8percentage points post-2020 (averaging an8.98.9percentage-point cost). Dropping sale-flagged quotes therefore helps the
standalone forecast on average in the stable pre-COVID period, driven by
the longest horizons, but hurts it across all horizon groups in the
post-COVID period, most severely at short horizons. Sale-driven price changes
add predictive signal during the inflation surge, while in stable times
they primarily inject noise that the baseline encoding has to absorb.Table F.5:Micro Forecast Percentage MAE Improvement Relative to the Univariate Forecast: Baseline versus Sales-Excluded InputPre-2020Post-2020hhBaselineSales-ExcludedBaselineSales-Excluded1-35.67-35.118.49-0.902-28.92-54.834.46-4.373-29.10-41.512.14-11.524-30.97-46.759.67-15.505-32.52-41.776.73-17.146-19.51-41.4920.31-12.627-17.33-27.5313.006.658-17.05-23.478.633.289-27.49-34.6714.639.0310-18.19-11.7510.43-10.3011-19.84-13.7616.04-5.6112-12.812.211.79-13.4713-10.32-14.3712.954.2514-0.13-6.1215.2810.4315-22.62-26.5214.277.9416-21.06-26.9811.037.7917-31.47-22.28-3.333.6318-18.48-4.390.829.9219-28.04-6.079.0710.5420-26.65-17.879.347.4521-32.13-10.1935.4231.7522-44.26-3.7232.2726.0223-36.87-10.6039.3335.4824-27.88-13.8926.4623.91

## F.3.2Three-Expert Online Learning

TableF.6reports
the percentage MAE improvement of the three-expert patient Fixed Share
combination (fixedα=0.02\alpha=0.02,η=0.5\eta=0.5, the main-text baseline)
relative to the univariate forecast, with the micro model trained on sales-included
(baseline) and sales-excluded micro input respectively, holding the macro
and univariate forecasts fixed. Averaging within the same four horizon groups,
the sales-excluded three-expert combination’s percentage MAE improvements are−2.5%-2.5\%,−0.9%-0.9\%,−3.5%-3.5\%, and+1.9%+1.9\%in the pre-2020 window and7.8%7.8\%,10.4%10.4\%,10.5%10.5\%, and23.7%23.7\%in the post-2020 window. The
corresponding gaps relative to the baseline three-expert combination are−3.7-3.7,+0.5+0.5,−1.8-1.8, and+4.8+4.8percentage points pre-2020 (averaging
a0.10.1percentage-point cost) and−4.2-4.2,−3.4-3.4,−1.4-1.4, and−1.5-1.5percentage points post-2020 (averaging a2.62.6percentage-point cost).
The post-COVID standalone gap therefore shrinks from8.98.9to2.62.6percentage points once the sales-excluded micro forecast is combined with
the macro and univariate forecasts through the Fixed Share combination. As with
the other robustness checks, the combination absorbs much of the

## F.4Sale-Filtered Micro (Nakamura–Steinsson Filter A) vs Baseline

The sale-filtered variant keeps the 11-feature encoding and CPI-weighted
aggregation but, instead of dropping sale-flagged quotes, replaces temporary
sale prices with the regular price inferred by theNakamura and Steinsson (2008)filter under its baseline parameterisation
(Filter A of TableA.2; AppendixA.4describes
the algorithm). Whereas the sales-excluded variant of
AppendixF.3removes the affected quotes, the filter
keeps them at an imputed regular price, so the comparison isolates the
consequences of imputing rather than deleting sale-driven price changes.
Because the filter’s rebound and modal-price rules condition on prices up toL​LLLmonths aftertt, the filtered panel is a regular-price measurement
exercise rather than a strictly real-time forecasting input.

## F.4.1Standalone Micro Forecast

TableF.7reports the percentage MAE
improvement of the standalone micro forecast relative to the univariate
forecast under sales-included (baseline) and sale-filtered micro input, for
all horizonsh=1,…,24h=1,\ldots,24. Averaging within four horizon groups (h=1h=1to66,77to1212,1313to1818, and1919to2424), the sale-filtered variant’s
percentage MAE improvements relative to the univariate forecast are−36.2%-36.2\%,−42.1%-42.1\%,−26.0%-26.0\%, and−29.4%-29.4\%in the pre-2020 window and−1.8%-1.8\%,−1.4%-1.4\%,6.7%6.7\%, and24.3%24.3\%in the post-2020 window. The corresponding
gaps relative to the baseline (sale-filtered minus baseline) are−6.8-6.8,−23.3-23.3,−8.6-8.6, and+3.2+3.2percentage points pre-2020 (averaging an8.98.9percentage-point cost) and−10.4-10.4,−12.1-12.1,−1.8-1.8, and−1.0-1.0percentage
points post-2020 (averaging a6.36.3percentage-point cost). Unlike the
sales-excluded variant, which helps the standalone forecast in the stable

## Appendix GAdditional Results

## G.1Macro Forecasts versus the Univariate Benchmark

While the primary focus of the paper is on microdata, we also document the
performance of the macro forecast, which displays an analogous
regime-dependent pattern.

## Visual evidence across forecast horizons.

FigureG.1plots both sets of forecasts against
realized inflation at horizonh=12h=12. In the pre-2020 panels, the univariate and
macro forecasts track realized inflation similarly, with the univariate forecast generally
remaining slightly closer. The post-2020 panel shows whyh=12h=12is one of the
macro forecast’s weakest post-2020 horizons: neither forecast anticipates the
2021–22 run-up, and the macro forecast stays flat through the surge, so the
univariate forecast retains a9.9%9.9\%MAE advantage at this horizon. The
macro forecast’s gains are instead concentrated at long horizons, where the
post-2020 improvements reach29%29\%to42%42\%(h=21h=21to2424;
TableG.1), as the univariate
forecast overshoots badly in 2023–24 at those horizons while the macro
forecast does not. FiguresD.7,D.8, andD.9report
theh=1h=1,h=6h=6, andh=24h=24forecasts.Figure G.1:Macro Forecast vs. Univariate Forecast:h=12h=12

Notes:Each panel plots 3-month trailing moving
averages of realized monthly inflation, the macro 12-month-ahead
forecast, and the univariate (SARIMA) 12-month-ahead forecast. Dates on
the x-axis are target months: a forecast datedttwas formed at origint−12t-12. The panels correspond to the 2015–2019 and 2020–2024 evaluation
windows.

## Aggregate evidence: pre-2020 versus post-2020 windows.

TableG.1reports the
percentage MAE improvement of the macro forecast relative to the univariate forecast
across the two evaluation windows, pre-2020 (2015–2019) and post-2020
(2020–2024), for all horizonsh=1,…,24h=1,\ldots,24;
FigureG.3visualizes the
same comparison as a heatmap.

Over the pre-2020 window, the macro forecast underperforms the univariate forecast at all
24 horizons; the smallest shortfall is5.2%5.2\%ath=13h=13. Averaging within
four horizon groups (h=1h=1to66,77to1212,1313to1818, and1919to2424), the percentage MAE losses are15.1%15.1\%,19.4%19.4\%,15.9%15.9\%, and13.5%13.5\%. Macro covariates add no
predictive value beyond the univariate benchmark in a stable-inflation
environment.

After 2020, the ranking changes. The macro forecast improves on the univariate forecast at 19
of the 24 horizons, with the exceptions ath=2h=2to55(between−1.5%-1.5\%and−3.8%-3.8\%) and ath=12h=12(−9.9%-9.9\%). Averaging within the same four
horizon groups, the post-COVID percentage MAE gains are−0.4%-0.4\%,9.4%9.4\%,6.4%6.4\%, and26.7%26.7\%, rising with the horizon and peaking at the longest
horizons, where the largest gain reaches41.9%41.9\%ath=23h=23.
The contrast between the two windows indicates that the value of macro data
is state dependent: limited during normal times but substantially higher
during periods of macroeconomic stress.Table G.1:Macro percent MAE improvement over SARIMA, pre- and post-COVID.hhPre-COVID % impr.Post-COVID % impr.1-25.197.862-22.25-3.143-14.44-3.824-11.02-3.095-5.59-1.516-11.861.107-10.2614.528-19.8016.599-25.0619.5310-21.2810.0811-19.135.3412-20.69-9.9213-5.243.5914-10.142.5115-23.7411.3016-25.624.1817-20.127.6618-10.269.1419-13.5410.0920-23.4611.4621-20.0733.5522-10.8934.1223-5.7341.8724-7.2829.18

## G.2Univariate + Macro Online Learning as a Benchmark without Microdata

To isolate the incremental contribution of microdata to the online-learning
step, we consider a two-expert benchmark that combines only the macro and
univariate forecasts, using the same patient Fixed Share baseline as in
Subsection4.3(η=0.5\eta=0.5,α=0.02\alpha=0.02). This exercise asks
whether a flexible combination of macro and
univariate forecasts can already replicate the gains that we attribute to
adding microdata.

## Results.

TableG.2shows that the two-expert benchmark tracks the univariate forecast before
2020 and improves on it broadly afterwards. In the pre-2020 window, the
percentage MAE improvements averaged within four horizon groups (h=1h=1to66,77to1212,1313to1818, and1919to2424) are1.1%1.1\%,−1.5%-1.5\%,−3.4%-3.4\%, and0.4%0.4\%, with positive gains at 9 of the 24 horizons and an
average gap of−0.9%-0.9\%. In the
post-2020 window, by contrast, the two-expert combination improves on the univariate forecast at
all 24 horizons, with group averages of8.8%8.8\%,12.9%12.9\%,9.6%9.6\%, and21.6%21.6\%, averaging13.2%13.2\%and strongest at the longest horizons. Fixed
Share combination of macro and univariate
information therefore captures a substantial share of the post-2020
forecasting signal.

## G.46-Group Shapley Values

## Construction.

We partition the micro variables, computed within each COICOP1 category and
then stacked across categories (except the monthly dummies, which are common
across categories), into six groups:
- 1.

the fraction of zero price changes;
- 2.

the mean price change;
- 3.

the higher moments of the price-change distribution;
- 4.

the deciles of the price-change distribution;
- 5.

the time since last change features;
- 6.

monthly dummies.

This decomposition is based on the augmented encoding rather than the baseline,
so it adds two feature groups that are absent from the main-text decomposition,
namely the higher moments of the price-change distribution and the hazard-style
statistics based on time since last adjustment. It lets us separate the
location of the price-change distribution (the mean), its coarse shape
(deciles), its higher moments, and the time-since-last-change features.Table G.4:6-Grouped Shapley Values for the Micro Expert, Averaged over 24 HorizonsFeature groupPre-2020Post-2020Fraction of zero price changes0.59640.0743Mean price change0.28530.7311Higher moments of price-change distribution-0.67740.2298Deciles of price-change distribution-0.08860.7128Time since last change features-0.8356-2.8658Monthly dummies0.18040.8718

Notes:Entries are grouped Shapley contributions of each feature group to the MAE gain
from adding the micro expert to the two-expert online-learning benchmark, which
combines the univariate and macro forecasts. Values are in percent of benchmark
MAE and are averaged over horizonsh=1,…,24h=1,\ldots,24within each evaluation
window. Positive entries reduce MAE; negative entries increase it.

TableG.4decomposes the augmented-encoding
micro expert. In the pre-COVID window, no feature
group makes a large positive contribution: the fraction of zero price changes
is the most positive at0.600.60, followed by the mean price change at0.290.29and
monthly dummies at0.180.18, while the time-since-last-change
features (−0.84-0.84), the higher moments (−0.68-0.68), and the deciles (−0.09-0.09)
are negative. This again points to limited
incremental forecasting content in microdata during the low and stable
inflation period.

In the post-COVID window, the positive contributions come from monthly
dummies (0.870.87), the mean price change (0.730.73), the deciles of the
price-change distribution (0.710.71), and, more modestly, the higher moments
(0.230.23); the fraction of zero price changes is essentially zero (0.070.07).
The
time-since-last-change features, however, contribute−2.87-2.87, more than
offsetting every positive group. The six contributions therefore sum to the
augmented-encoding three-expert improvement over the two-expert
benchmark, which is negative after 2020 (−0.25-0.25percent). The augmented
encoding therefore underperforms the baseline encoding after COVID, and the
shortfall is accounted for by the hazard-style timing block rather than by
the distributional statistics, consistent with the encoding-level robustness
comparison in Subsection5.6.Figure G.5:6-Horizon Moving Average of Per-Horizon 6-Group Shapley Values(a)Pre-COVID(b)Post-COVID

Notes:The figure reports 6-group Shapley
contributions for the incremental value of the augmented-encoding micro
forecast in the three-expert combined forecast relative to the two-expert
benchmark that combines the univariate and macro forecasts. Values are smoothed
across adjacent horizons using a 6-horizon moving average. Positive
values indicate reductions in MAE from the corresponding feature group;
negative values indicate increases in MAE. The horizontal line marks a
zero contribution.

FigureG.5shows the same pattern at the
horizon level. Before COVID, the per-horizon contributions are small at short
and medium horizons, and the deciles group deteriorates steeply at the
longest horizons, echoing the long-horizon losses of the baseline
decomposition. After COVID, monthly dummies are positive at every horizon,
the mean price change strengthens toward long horizons, and the deciles are
positive at short and medium horizons. The time-since-last-change features
are strongly negative at every horizon in the post-COVID window, reaching
below−4-4at medium horizons, and are negative at most horizons before COVID
as well. Taken together, the 6-group decomposition suggests that the
post-COVID forecasting value of microdata comes from the location and shape
of the price-change distribution together with seasonal timing, while the
hazard-style duration statistics actively hurt.

This subsection reports a coarser 4-group Shapley decomposition. It
partitions the same baseline feature set as the 5-group specification in
Section6, but instead of separating the center and the
tails of the price-change distribution, it keeps the mean price change and
the deciles as single groups:
- 1.

the fraction of zero price changes;
- 2.

the mean price change;
- 3.

the deciles of the price-change distribution;
- 4.

monthly dummies.Table G.5:4-Group Shapley Values for the Micro Expert, Averaged over 24 HorizonsFeature groupPre-2020Post-2020Fraction of zero price changes0.1254-0.0093Mean price change-0.22160.9672Deciles of price-change distribution-0.60090.6423Monthly dummies0.29931.3076

Notes:Entries are grouped Shapley contributions of each feature group to the MAE gain

window. Positive entries reduce MAE; negative entries increase it.

TableG.5shows that, in the
pre-COVID window, the contributions of the deciles of the price-change
distribution and the mean price change are negative, with values−0.60-0.60and−0.22-0.22, respectively, while monthly dummies (0.300.30) and the
fraction of zero price changes (0.130.13) are mildly positive. Thus, before
COVID
the grouped micro contributions are small overall, with no group delivering a

partition the same baseline feature set, their contributions sum to the same

In the post-COVID window, the largest positive contribution comes from monthly

deciles of the price-change distribution at0.640.64; the fraction of zero price

across adjacent horizons using a 6-horizon moving average. Positive
values indicate reductions in MAE from the corresponding feature group;
negative values indicate increases in MAE. The horizontal line marks a
zero contribution.

FigureG.6confirms the same broad
message at the horizon level: before COVID the contributions are small at
short and medium horizons, with the losses concentrated in the deciles group
at the longest horizons; after
COVID, mean price changes, monthly indicators, and, at short and medium
horizons, the price-change deciles are the
main positive contributors.
